A Compressible Miles Instability for Wind-Wave
Generation in a Neutral Atmosphere
Tianyi Li∗
September 2026
Abstract
We study the normal-mode stability of a flat interface between compressible Euler flow
in the air and incompressible Euler flow in the water, including gravity and surface tension.
The base state is quiescent water beneath a hydrostatic, constant-entropy shear flow U(z)
with U(0) = 0, so the atmosphere is neutrally stratified. For a small ratio ε of the air
density at the interface to the water density, and for uniformly subsonic wave-relative flow,
we establish a compressible analog of the critical-layer instability of Miles, extending the
rigorous incompressible theory of B¨uhler, Shatah, Walsh, and Zeng. For a real phase speed
cr, the singular coefficient at a regular critical level U(zc) = cr has a residue proportional
to Q = U ′′ + gU ′/a2 = ¯ρ0(U ′/¯ρ0)′, with gravitational acceleration g, sound speed a, and
normalized base-state air density ¯ρ0. Thus the gradient of specific vorticity replaces the
curvature U ′′ of the incompressible theory.
We prove a limiting absorption principle at regular, non-resonant phase speeds and
a residue formula for the imaginary part of the boundary value Z+
a (k, cr) of the air-side
pressure response at wavenumber k > 0. Let ck > 0 be the phase speed of the free
gravity–capillary wave. If Im Z+
a (k, ck) > 0, then, for sufficiently small ε, the dispersion
relation has exactly one unstable root cε in a neighborhood of ck that does not depend on
ε. A sufficient condition is Q ≤0 at every critical level and Q < 0 at the highest one.
As the Mach number M →0, Z+
a converges to its incompressible counterpart at rate
O(M 2). Under the corresponding incompressible sign condition, the unstable root satisfies
|cε(M) −cε(0)| ≤CεM 2 with C independent of ε and M.
Keywords: wind-wave generation, compressible Euler equations, free-boundary problems,
spectral instability.
MSC 2020: 76E17, 76E19, 76E05, 76B15, 35Q31, 35R35.

1
Introduction
Wind waves are the surface waves of the ocean that the wind generates. When wind blows
over the sea, it transfers momentum and energy to the water, and the surface waves grow.
Understanding this transfer is a classical problem of geophysical fluid dynamics, and it matters
in practice. The wind input is the source term that drives operational wave forecasting models
2


[28, 25], the wave field sets the roughness and the drag of the sea surface felt by the atmosphere
[49], and wave breaking controls the exchange of gases, including carbon dioxide, between the
ocean and the atmosphere [14].
The generation process has several stages, and the classical theories treat them separately.
Phillips [47] described the initial excitation of a calm surface by the turbulent pressure
fluctuations of the wind. Miles [45] described the subsequent exponential growth of a wave
through its coupling to the sheared mean wind. Benjamin [5] computed the normal and
tangential stresses that a laminar shear flow exerts on a wavy boundary. Lighthill [34] interpreted
Miles’s mechanism physically through the vortex force at the critical layer. Townsend [50]
included the variation of the turbulent Reynolds stresses in a deep turbulent boundary layer
over waves, and Belcher and Hunt [4] derived an asymptotic theory with four layers for
turbulent shear flow over slowly moving waves, using rapid-distortion theory for the outer flow.
These theories rest on formal linearization and asymptotic matching. A rigorous mathematical
foundation for the incompressible Miles problem was given only recently [8].
In Phillips’s theory a pressure component traveling at the phase speed of a free surface
wave excites that wave resonantly. The theory distinguishes an initial stage from a principal
stage of linear growth of the mean-square displacement. Li and Shen [31, 32] confirmed both
stages by direct numerical simulation, developed a random-sweeping pressure–wave model for
the principal stage, and obtained direct evidence of the initial-stage resonance.
Miles’s theory describes what happens once the wave feeds back on the air. Because the
ratio ε ≈1.2 × 10−3 of the air density at the sea surface to the water density is small, a
water wave of horizontal wavenumber k travels, to leading order, at the phase speed ck of the
free gravity–capillary wave, that is, of the wave that the water would carry if the air density
were zero. The wave interacts most strongly with the wind at the height zc where the wind
speed U(z) equals the phase speed, U(zc) = ck. This height is the critical level, and the thin
region around it is the critical layer. Miles’s formula gives an O(ε) growth rate whose sign, for
incompressible air, is determined by the curvature U′′(zc) of the wind profile at the critical
level, with growth when U′′(zc) < 0. Here O(ε) denotes a quantity whose absolute value is
at most a constant multiple of ε, with the constant independent of ε, and we use O(·) in the
same way for the other small quantities below. The mechanism has been confirmed by field
and laboratory measurements [24, 9, 48] and enters the wind-input source terms of operational
wave models [25, 28]. The present paper treats this mechanism and asks how atmospheric
compressibility modifies it.
B¨uhler, Shatah, Walsh, and Zeng [8] (BSWZ) gave the first rigorous proof of Miles’s
criterion. They derived the linearization of the two-phase Euler equations about an arbitrary
shear flow and proved the criterion in the presence of surface tension and of an interfacial
vortex sheet, a jump of the tangential velocity across the interface. Their result therefore
covers both the Kelvin–Helmholtz theory, in which such a jump drives the growth of the waves,
and the quasi-laminar theory. Here quasi-laminar means that turbulence sets the mean wind
profile, while the wave-induced turbulent stresses and the instantaneous turbulent fluctuations
are neglected. Subsequent rigorous work treats the full spectral picture of the two-phase
problem [37], the linearized dynamics of one-phase free-surface shear flows [39, 38], and circular
geometries [21].
All of these rigorous results take the air to be incompressible with constant density. At
the Mach numbers of winds over the sea this is a natural approximation, but the size of
the correction it neglects has not been quantified within the wind-wave eigenvalue problem.
We are not aware of a rigorous normal-mode theory that combines a compressible gas, an
incompressible liquid, a sheared wind, and a Miles critical layer.
We consider the full compressible Euler equations in the air and the incompressible
Euler equations in water of finite depth, coupled through a free interface, with gravitational
acceleration g > 0 and surface tension coefficient σ ≥0. The horizontal coordinate is x and the
3


vertical coordinate is z, measured upward from the undisturbed interface z = 0. The air has a
general equation of state p = P(ρ, S), which gives the pressure p as a function of the density ρ
and the specific entropy S. In an inviscid gas without heat conduction, each fluid parcel carries
its specific entropy unchanged. Below, entropy means specific entropy. The undisturbed air
and water depths are ha > 0 and hw > 0, and the top and bottom boundaries z = ha and
z = −hw are flat and impermeable, so the vertical velocity vanishes there. The base state, the
undisturbed flow about which we linearize, consists of water of constant density ρw at rest
beneath a flat interface and a wind U(z) in hydrostatic air with U(0) = 0 and constant entropy.
Its air density is ρ0(z), and the small parameter of the problem is the ratio ε = ρ0(0)/ρw of
the air density at the interface to the water density.
Constant entropy is the main physical restriction of this paper, and we explain what it
means. A parcel of air displaced vertically expands or contracts adiabatically. If it arrives
lighter or heavier than its new surroundings, buoyancy pushes it back and it oscillates. The
frequency of this oscillation is the Brunt–V¨ais¨al¨a frequency N, and such oscillations propagating
through the air are internal gravity waves. When the entropy of the base state is constant with
height, a displaced parcel always has the density of its surroundings, so there is no restoring
force, N vanishes, and the atmosphere is neutrally stratified. In a hydrostatic atmosphere,
neutrality is equivalent to a density profile that decreases at the rate set by the sound speed,
N2 = −g

where a(z) is the sound speed of the base state and the prime denotes d/ dz. The restriction
has a mathematical and a physical justification. A neutral atmosphere carries no internal
gravity waves, so the only singular behavior of the normal-mode equation at a critical level is
the one already present in Rayleigh’s equation for incompressible shear flow, a simple pole. In a
stratified atmosphere, the normal modes instead satisfy an equation of Taylor–Goldstein type,
whose solutions near a critical level behave like powers of the distance to it, with exponents
that depend on the Richardson number N2/U′2 at the critical level (Remark 4.6). Physically,
the logarithmic wind profile that wave models use for the wind input is the profile of a
neutral surface layer [25], so neutral stratification is the standard description of the lowest
tens of meters over the sea, where the critical levels of wind waves lie. Compared with the
incompressible theory, two effects then remain in the air equations. The sound speed is finite,
and the base-state density decreases with height at the hydrostatic rate ρ′
0/ρ0 = −g/a2.
1.1
Main results
The central question of the present study is how Miles’s critical-layer instability and its criterion
change when the air is a compressible gas. We answer it for uniformly subsonic wave-relative
flow, which includes the low-Mach-number regime of winds over the sea without being restricted
to it. Uniformly subsonic means that the speed of the wind relative to the wave stays below
a constant fraction, smaller than one, of the sound speed (Hypothesis (H4)). The instability
persists, with the curvature U′′(zc) replaced by Q(zc) = U′′(zc) + (g/a2(zc)) U′(zc), the density-
weighted gradient of specific vorticity given in Equation (4). The incompressible theory is
recovered as the Mach number M tends to zero, where M is the ratio of a reference wind speed
to the sound speed at the interface. This convergence holds for the unstable root itself, at the
rate M2 (§7). In a model regime of strong compressibility, Q(zc) > 0 although U′′ < 0, and
the Miles bifurcation is absent, that is, no growing mode emerges from the free water wave as
the density ratio ε increases from zero.
A normal mode is a solution of the linearized equations in which every perturbation field is
the product of a function of the height z and the factor eik(x−ct), with horizontal wavenumber
k > 0 and complex phase speed c. The mode grows exponentially in time exactly when
4


Im c > 0, at the rate k Im c. Here Re and Im denote the real and imaginary parts of a complex
number. The proof reduces each normal mode to an ordinary differential equation for the
vertical-velocity amplitude, which becomes singular at critical levels as the imaginary part of c
tends to zero. It then defines the solution at a real phase speed as the limit of the solutions
with Im c > 0, which is a limiting absorption principle, and it locates the unstable root near
the free wave speed ck with Rouch´e’s theorem. This theorem states that two functions that are
holomorphic, that is, complex differentiable, on an open set containing a simple closed curve
and its interior have equally many zeros inside the curve when their difference is smaller in
modulus than one of them on the curve. Here zeros are counted with multiplicity, so that a
simple zero, at which the derivative does not vanish, counts once.
Write ¯ρ0(z) = ρ0(z)/(ερw) = ρ0(z)/ρ0(0) for the base-state air density normalized by its
value at the interface, so that ¯ρ0(0) = 1. For a horizontal wavenumber k > 0 let ck be the
positive free gravity–capillary wave speed defined in Equation (24), which depends on g, σ, ρw,
k, and the water depth hw. A critical level zc is regular when U′(zc)̸ = 0, so that the wind
speed crosses the phase speed with nonzero shear. The main theorem treats a monotone wind
with one regular critical level.
Theorem 1.1. Let U ∈C4([0, ha]) with U(0) = 0 and U′ > 0. Let the neutrally stratified
atmosphere (¯ρ0, a) satisfy the regularity and hydrostatic assumptions of §2.5. Let k > 0 be a
horizontal wavenumber such that the free wave speed ck at this wavenumber lies in U((0, ha)),
so that there is exactly one critical level zc ∈(0, ha) with U(zc) = ck, and such that the
wave-relative flow U −c is uniformly subsonic for c near ck. Assume
Q(zc) = U′′(zc) +
g
a2(zc) U′(zc) < 0.
(2)
Then there are ε0 > 0 and r0 > 0 with the following properties for every air–water density
ratio ε ∈(0, ε0). The linearized problem has exactly one unstable phase-speed root cε in the set
{c ∈C : |c−ck| < r0, Im c > 0}, and the space of normal modes at this root is one-dimensional.
The root satisfies cε = ck + εc1 + O(ε2(1 + log(1/ε))) with an explicit c1, and the growth rate
is γε = k Im cε > 0. The number λε = −ikcε is an isolated, algebraically simple eigenvalue of
the closed, densely defined operator Lk of Theorem 3.5, the linearized evolution operator at
wavenumber k. If, in addition, the equation of state satisfies the thermodynamic regularity in
Equation (188), then Lk is the linearization of the nonlinear two-phase problem in the sense of
Theorem 3.5.
Proof. This is Corollary 6.8.
The last part of the theorem addresses a point that formal linearization does not settle. In
a free-boundary problem the fluid domains move with the solution, so the term “linearized
operator” is not well defined until the problem is posed on fixed function spaces. BSWZ [8, §2]
raised this point for the incompressible problem. The dynamically correct object is the Fr´echet
derivative of the equations after they have been transported to fixed reference domains. Here
the Fr´echet derivative at a state is the linear map that approximates how the expressions in the
equations change under a small perturbation of the state, with an error that is small compared
with the perturbation. Appendix C carries out this transport, shows that the derivative exists
under the thermodynamic regularity in Equation (188), and shows that, after a change of
variables which removes the apparent perturbation produced by moving the coordinate system,
this derivative coincides with the linearized system obtained by differentiating the equations
at fixed points. The new unknown after this change of variables is called the good unknown
[2]. The operator Lk is then the closure of this linear system in L2, which is the standard way
to turn a differential operator into a closed operator with a well-defined spectrum. Here L2
denotes the space of square-integrable functions of the height, defined precisely in Remark 3.2.
5


Densely defined means that the domain of Lk, the subspace of states on which Lk is defined,
is dense in the L2-based state space, that is, every state is a limit, in the norm of this space,
of states in the domain. Section 3.3 defines closed operators, the closure, the spectrum, the
resolvent, the Riesz projection, and algebraic simplicity. The theorem therefore says that the
growing mode is an eigenvalue of the actual derivative of the nonlinear problem. A formally
linearized system alone would not give this. The eigenvalue is isolated, so no other point of
the spectrum lies near it, and algebraically simple, that is, the Riesz projection onto it has
a one-dimensional range, so the mode is unique up to a constant factor. Theorem 3.5 and
Proposition C.9 also give an explicit bound on the resolvent (λ −Lk)−1 near it.
We now describe the objects in the theorem. Consider a normal mode with Im c̸ = 0 and
write the perturbation of the vertical air velocity as w(z)eik(x−ct), so that w is the vertical-
velocity amplitude, a function of height alone. Because the base entropy is constant and
each parcel conserves its entropy, the entropy perturbation of such a mode vanishes, and the
remaining air amplitudes, the horizontal velocity, the density, and the pressure, are recovered
from w (Lemma 4.1 and Proposition 4.3). The amplitude w satisfies

by parts over an interval free of critical and sonic levels move the differential operator from
one function to the other when both functions vanish at the ends of the interval. It is not a
Sturm–Liouville eigenvalue problem in the usual sense, in which the eigenvalue multiplies a
given weight function, because the phase speed c enters the coefficients ¯A and G/V nonlinearly
through V = U −c. At a regular critical level, one has

Thus the coefficient G/V has at most a simple pole in z at the critical level. Its residue at
zc, the coefficient of 1/(z −zc) in its expansion near zc, is ¯ρ0(zc)Q(zc)/U′(zc). The identity
Q = ¯ρ0(U′/¯ρ0)′ identifies the critical-layer coefficient with ¯ρ0 times the vertical gradient of the
specific vorticity U′/¯ρ0, the vorticity per unit mass. In compressible boundary-layer stability
theory the corresponding coefficient is (ρ0U′)′, the quantity in the generalized inflection-point
criterion of Lees and Lin [29, 41]. The two density weightings differ because gravity enters
the present problem twice, and Remark 4.5 explains the relation. The same specific-vorticity
gradient controls the momentum that a wave deposits at a critical level in compressible
wave–mean-flow theory [19, 3, 7].
The air acts on the interface through a single complex number for each pair (k, c). Suppose
the interface is displaced by ˆζ eik(x−ct), so that ˆζ is the interface displacement amplitude. The
air-pressure perturbation evaluated on the displaced interface includes the change −ρ0(0)gˆζ of
the base-state pressure over the displacement. This is the Lagrangian pressure perturbation,
and it is proportional to ˆζ. The air-side pressure response Za(k, c) of Equation (53) is this
Lagrangian pressure perturbation divided by the surface air density ρ0(0) = ερw and by
the displacement amplitude ˆζ. It is the pressure that the air exerts on the interface per
unit of interface displacement, divided by the surface air density, and it depends on the
normalized profiles (¯ρ0, a, U) but not on ε.
The water side enters through the function
Dw(k, c) = kc2 coth(khw) −g −σk2/ρw of Equation (23), whose positive zero is the free wave
speed ck. For Im c > 0, a normal mode exists exactly when the water-wave dynamics at phase
6


speed c is balanced by the air pressure, Dw(k, c) = εZa(k, c) (Proposition 3.3). Since the air is
light, the right-hand side is O(ε), so roots near ±ck are O(ε) perturbations of ±ck, and the
growth rate of the root near ck is set by the imaginary part of Za there.
For Im c > 0, Equation (3) has no singularity, because V = U −c does not vanish, and
Za(k, ·) is a holomorphic function of c except at the values of c for which the air solution
vanishing at the top boundary also vanishes at the interface, w(0; k, c) = 0. At such values
the normalization by the interface displacement is impossible and Za has a pole, so Za(k, ·)
is meromorphic on the upper half-plane, that is, holomorphic except at isolated poles. The
unstable root we seek has a small positive imaginary part, so we need the behavior of Za(k, c)
as c = cr + iδ tends to the real phase speed cr, with δ > 0 decreasing to zero. At real phase
speeds, the equation for w is singular at every critical level, where V = 0, and its solution is
not defined directly. The limiting absorption principle of scattering theory resolves this. It
defines the boundary value Z+
a (k, cr) as the limit of Za(k, cr +iδ) as δ ↓0. Physically, the small
imaginary part δ is a weak exponential growth in time, and the limit selects the solution that a
slowly growing wave sees. In the classical theory this is the rule of Lin and Miles for the branch
of the logarithm at the critical level [45, 17]. A real phase speed cr is regular when every zero
of U −cr is simple, and it is non-resonant, in the sense of Hypothesis (H5), when the limiting
air solution does not vanish at the interface. Resonance would mean that cr is also the phase
speed of a free oscillation of the air layer alone, between a flat interface held at rest and the
top boundary, and then Za is singular there. For a regular, non-resonant cr satisfying uniform
subsonicity, Theorem 5.8 proves that the limit exists, at the rate O

Equation (5) has a physical meaning. Write ξ for the vertical displacement amplitude of the
air parcels, ¯q for the normalized pressure amplitude, and an asterisk for complex conjugation.
The quantity J = Im(¯qξ∗), the correlation between the pressure and the slope of the displaced
material surfaces, is the vertical flux of horizontal wave momentum, called the wave-induced
form stress in the wind-wave literature and the Eliassen–Palm flux in the wave–mean-flow
literature [19]. Here a material surface is a surface that consists of the same air parcels at all
times, such as the surface formed by the parcels that lie at one height in the base state. For a
neutral mode, one with real phase speed c that neither grows nor decays, this flux is constant
in height except at the critical levels, where it jumps. The jth summand in Equation (5) is
the jump of J across zj (Lemma 4.7 and Theorem 5.8), that is, the momentum that the wave
deposits in the mean wind at that critical level. The balance has the same form as in the
incompressible theory, with U′′ replaced by Q. For the criterion to be effective, the limiting
solution must not vanish at a critical level, and a monotonicity argument shows that it does
not vanish at the highest one (Lemma 5.13).

a (k, ck) + O(ε2(1 + log(1/ε))). The
sign condition ckQ(zj) ≤0 at every critical level, with strict inequality at the highest one,
is sufficient for instability (Corollary 6.7). For a monotone increasing wind with one critical
level this becomes Q(zc) < 0, as stated in Theorem 1.1. If ck lies outside the range of U, so
that there is no critical level, the root near ck is instead real and simple, and it is given by
a convergent power series in ε (Theorem 6.2). Hence the critical layer is the only source of
growth near ck.
To quantify the incompressible approximation, Section 7 considers neutral atmospheres
parametrized by the Mach number M, with sound speed aM = a∗/M, where a∗is a refer-
ence speed of the order of the wind and wave speeds. Theorem 7.1 proves |Z+,M
a
(k, cr) −
Z+,inc
a
(k, cr)| ≤CM2 uniformly for cr in compact sets of regular, non-resonant phase speeds
and on a closed neighborhood of ck in the upper half-plane that does not depend on M.
Here C is independent of M, and the superscripts M and inc mark the atmosphere with
Mach number M and the incompressible problem. A compact set of real phase speeds is a
closed and bounded set. Hence the leading coefficient c1(M) of the compressible problem
satisfies c1(M) = c1(0) + O(M2), with c1(0) its incompressible value. Under the incompressible
sign condition, the unstable roots of the compressible and incompressible problems satisfy
|cε(M) −cε(0)| ≤CεM2 at each wavenumber k, with C independent of ε and M. This is the
quantitative form of the statement that the incompressible theory is accurate for wind waves.
For wind profiles that increase with height with shear decreasing upward, U′ > 0 and
U′′ < 0, as for the logarithmic profile, the hydrostatic term (g/a2)U′ in Q is positive and shifts
the criterion from U′′ < 0 to Q < 0. For critical levels at the heights typical of wind waves
over the sea, meters to tens of meters above the surface, the shift is negligible (§8.2). For the
bounded shear-layer profile U = U∞(1 −e−z/L), with limiting speed U∞and shear depth L, in
the neutral constant-sound-speed atmosphere, the condition a2 < gL gives Q > 0 although
U′′ < 0. At each admissible wavenumber the local O(ε) Miles bifurcation from ck is then absent
(Proposition 8.2). This regime requires a sound speed far below that of air. The example
shows that the sign criterion of the incompressible theory does not extend to a compressible
atmosphere in general.
1.2
Methods and relation to prior work
The critical-layer argument is built around the variable ¯χ = ik¯q, the horizontal pressure-gradient
amplitude scaled by the surface air density. Unlike the equation for w, its first-order equation
is regular at U = cr. Variation of constants then reconstructs w from ¯χ and isolates a single
logarithmic term with coefficient Q/U′. A contraction argument for a single integral equation
gives the local expansion near each critical level and estimates that are uniform as Im c ↓0.
Such an argument writes the solution as the unique fixed point of a map that multiplies
every distance by at most a constant factor smaller than one, the contraction constant. The
compressible theory rests on two exact cancellations. The hydrostatic terms cancel in the flux
identity of Lemma 4.7, and the singular terms cancel in the derivative of the monotonicity
functional of Lemma 5.12. These identities yield the residue formula and the sign criterion.
Rouch´e’s theorem on a neighborhood of ck in the upper half-plane, applied after an estimate
that places all nearby roots within O(ε) of ck, proves existence, uniqueness, and simplicity of
the unstable root. For ¯A = ¯ρ0 = 1 and b = 0, the same argument recovers the incompressible
critical-layer theory of [8, §4]. Appendix C supplies the formulation on fixed reference domains,
its linearization, and the construction of the operator Lk.
8


Maleewong and Grimshaw [42] study internal-wave radiation in a density-stratified incom-
pressible atmosphere, a mechanism absent when N2 = 0. Compressible vortex-sheet results
[46, 12, 52] treat piecewise-constant flows without critical layers. Lees and Lin [29] and Mack
[41] derive the local compressible critical-layer algebra for boundary layers over solid walls,
with no free surface or gravity. Other related results include compressible Rayleigh–Taylor
normal modes [22], compressible Miles–Howard and semicircle theorems [10, 18], and viscous
compressible–incompressible free-boundary theory [15]. The incompressible necessity and
positivity arguments have precedents in [8, 37], while the low-Mach result is related to the
incompressible-limit theory [27, 44, 36, 16, 40].
A complementary numerical approach keeps atmospheric compressibility in wave-phase-
resolved simulations. Zhu et al. [53] introduced a moving lower boundary in WRF–LES and
validated the method for laminar and turbulent flow over prescribed monochromatic waves. Li
et al. [33] extended the method to prescribed broadband wave fields with different propagation
directions and directional spreading. These calculations solve the compressible atmospheric
equations with turbulence modeling while prescribing the wave surface. The present paper
treats the inviscid two-phase free-boundary eigenvalue problem, in which the interface motion
and the air pressure are coupled.
1.3
Modeling assumptions and scope
Neutral stratification removes internal gravity waves (Remark 4.6). The impermeable top
boundary replaces the outgoing acoustic radiation condition, which lets sound waves leave the
air layer upward, by a reflecting boundary, so the free acoustic oscillations of the air layer occur
only at a discrete set of frequencies. In the uniform-wind example its effect is exponentially
small when the top boundary lies several decay lengths above the interface, and this decay
length grows as the speed of the wind relative to the wave approaches the sound speed (§8.1).
Uniform subsonicity excludes sonic levels and hence the higher Mack modes of compressible
boundary layers, which are acoustic waves trapped in a region where the flow relative to the
wave is supersonic [41]. The condition U(0) = 0 excludes the vortex-sheet Kelvin–Helmholtz
mechanism at the interface [8]. Surface tension is included and enters the analysis at each
wavenumber through ck. Viscosity, turbulence closures, oblique three-dimensional modes, and
nonlinear instability are not considered.
1.4
Outline
We begin in Section 2 with the nonlinear two-phase problem and its laminar states. Section 3
derives the linearized equations and reduces them to a normal-mode problem. In Section 4, we
obtain the displacement–pressure formulation, the flux identities, and the holomorphic form of
the dispersion relation used in the instability analysis.
Section 5 establishes limiting absorption at the critical layers and derives the residue formula
for the boundary value of the pressure response. Section 6 first proves the necessary condition
for instability and then uses these critical-layer results to prove the main instability theorem.
Section 7 recovers the incompressible theory in the low-Mach limit. Section 8 discusses uniform
wind, logarithmic and shear-layer profiles, and a regime of strong compressibility in which the
Miles bifurcation does not occur.
Appendix A contains the thermodynamic identities and the general stratified equations.
Appendix B gives the technical estimates for the critical-layer analysis and the low-Mach limit.
Appendix C develops the fixed-domain formulation and establishes the operator interpretation
of the normal-mode results.
9


2
The nonlinear two-phase system and its laminar states
This section formulates the problem studied in this paper. We write the nonlinear equations for
compressible air over incompressible water, identify the steady laminar states about which we
linearize, introduce the air–water density ratio as the small parameter, and list the hypotheses
used in the rest of the paper.
2.1
Geometry and variables
Let ha > 0 and hw > 0 denote the undisturbed air and water depths. We work in two spatial
dimensions (x, z) ∈Tk0 ×R, where Tk0 = R/(2π/k0)Z and k0 > 0. In the normal-mode analysis
below, k > 0 denotes the horizontal wavenumber of the surface wave at the air–water interface
and the associated perturbations in the air and water. In the present study, we assume that
the horizontal domain is periodic, and hence we consider a single wavenumber k = nk0 with a
positive integer n. The same analysis may apply when the horizontal domain lies in x ∈R, in
which case k can be any positive real number. Gravity is −gez, where g > 0 and ez = (0, 1) is
the upward unit vector. At time t the interface is the graph
Γ(t) = {(x, z) : z = η(x, t)},
(7)
with air in Ωa(t) = {η(x, t) < z < ha} and water in Ωw(t) = {−hw < z < η(x, t)}. The
boundaries at z = ha and z = −hw are flat and impermeable, so the vertical velocity vanishes
there, see Equation (12) below.
We call the upper boundary z = ha the top boundary
and z = −hw the bottom boundary. The air velocity is denoted by ua = (ua, wa). The
thermodynamic state of the air is described by the density ρa > 0 and specific entropy Sa,
which determine the pressure pa through the equation of state. The water is assumed to be
incompressible and has constant density ρw > 0, velocity uw = (uw, ww), and pressure pw. The
interface elevation is η.

the air is Dw(k, c) = 0, where

Hypothesis 2.3 below summarizes the assumptions used in the present study. In Hypothe-
sis 2.3, Hypotheses (H1)–(H2) specify the regularity and interface velocity of the base state.
Hypotheses (H3)–(H5) are used to analyze the air-side pressure response at real phase speeds
near ck. The surface-tension condition in Hypothesis (H6) allows both gravity waves and
gravity–capillary waves in the current analysis.
Hypothesis 2.3.
(H1) The base-state wind velocity U, normalized air density ¯ρ0, and sound speed a satisfy
U ∈C4([0, ha]),
¯ρ0, a ∈C3([0, ha]).
(25)
The density and sound speed are positive on [0, ha] and satisfy the neutrality relation
in Equation (15).
(H2) The horizontal velocity of the base wind vanishes at the interface,
U(0) = 0.
(26)
Because the water is at rest in the base state, this condition makes the base velocity
continuous across the air–water interface.
(H3) The critical levels associated with the reference phase speed ck are the heights at which
the base wind velocity satisfies U(z) = ck. Every such level in [0, ha] lies in (0, ha) and
satisfies U′(z)̸ = 0. We call these regular critical levels and, when present, denote them
by z1 < · · · < zm.
(H4) The wind velocity relative to a wave of real phase speed c is uniformly subsonic near ck.
More precisely, there exists a constant δ0 > 0 such that
a2(z) −(U(z) −c)2 ≥δ0 a2(z)
(27)
for every height z ∈[0, ha] and every real phase speed c in a neighborhood of ck. We
call this condition uniform subsonicity.
(H5) Let w∗(z; cr) denote the limiting vertical air-velocity amplitude constructed in Proposi-
tion 5.7 as the complex phase speed c = cr + iδ approaches cr with δ ↓0. The solution
is normalized at the upper boundary by
w∗(ha; cr) = 0,
∂zw∗(ha; cr) = 1.
(28)
We require its value at the air–water interface to satisfy
w∗(0; cr)̸ = 0
(29)
for every real phase speed cr in a neighborhood of ck. We call this condition non-
resonance of the air boundary-value problem.
It excludes a nontrivial limiting air
solution, that is, one that is not identically zero, with both the interface and the upper
boundary held fixed at the phase speeds under consideration.
13


(H6) The surface tension coefficient at the air–water interface satisfies
σ ≥0.
(30)
Remark 2.4. These hypotheses allow us to determine the air-side pressure response near the
free water-wave speed ck and use it to study the effect of wind on the wave. The regularity of
U, ¯ρ0, and a in Hypothesis (H1) is needed to differentiate the coefficients of the air equations
and to estimate how their solutions vary with the wave speed. The neutrality relation ensures
hydrostatic balance and gives N2 = 0. Since the water is at rest in the base state, the condition
U(0) = 0 in Hypothesis (H2) makes the horizontal air and water velocities equal at the
undisturbed interface, excluding a jump in tangential velocity. The critical levels in Hypothesis
(H3) are the heights where the wind speed matches the reference wave speed, U(zj) = ck. The
requirement U′(zj)̸ = 0 ensures that these heights move smoothly as the wave speed varies.
It also ensures that the coefficient ¯ρ0k2 + G/V in Equation (3) has at most a simple pole at
each critical level, with residue proportional to Q(zj), which may be zero. Hypothesis (H4)
prevents the denominator a2 −(U −c)2 in the air equations from vanishing for real phase
speeds near ck. Hypothesis (H5) concerns the limiting vertical velocity amplitude w∗, obtained
by normalizing the air solution at the upper boundary. Since w∗(0; cr)̸ = 0, the solution can
be rescaled to satisfy the kinematic condition at the moving water surface, and the resulting
pressure response is finite. If w∗(0; cr) = 0, the limiting air problem instead admits a nontrivial
solution with the interface held fixed. When this zero occurs at cr = ck, the uncoupled air and
water problems share the phase speed ck at ε = 0, which is the resonance that the present
perturbation argument excludes (Remark 4.10). Non-resonance still allows critical levels where
U(z) = cr. Finally, Hypothesis (H6) includes both σ = 0, corresponding to gravity waves, and
σ > 0, corresponding to gravity–capillary waves.
Remark 2.5. For a complex phase speed c with Im c̸ = 0, the quantities V = U −c and a2 −V 2
are nonzero at every height, so the air normal-mode equation has no singular coefficients
(Lemma 5.1). Hypotheses (H1)–(H2) are therefore sufficient to construct the holomorphic
dispersion function E of Definition 4.8 and identify its zeros with the eigenvalues λ = −ikc of the
operator Lk of Theorem 3.5 with Re λ̸ = 0. The additional Hypotheses (H3)–(H5) are used to
control the air solution as c approaches a real phase speed near ck. This limiting analysis gives
the estimates needed for the application of Rouch´e’s theorem that proves instability in §6. In
some cases, the non-resonance condition in Hypothesis (H5) follows from the other assumptions
and need not be checked separately. Specifically, if the critical-level values Q(zj) are all
nonnegative or all nonpositive, with Q(zm)̸ = 0 at the highest critical level, then Lemma 5.13
implies Hypothesis (H5). This criterion is used in Corollaries 6.7 and 6.8 and Proposition 8.2.
Hypotheses (H4) and (H5) need only be checked at ck. Since [0, ha] is compact and U and a
are continuous, Hypothesis (H4) holds exactly when (U(z) −ck)2 < a2(z) for every z ∈[0, ha].
Hypothesis (H5) holds when w∗(0; ck)̸ = 0, because w∗(0; cr) depends continuously on cr near
ck by Proposition 5.7, which does not use Hypothesis (H5).
3
Linearization and normal-mode reduction
We linearize Equations (8)–(12) about the laminar state in Equation (13) and then seek normal
modes of the resulting equations. This reduces the coupled air–water problem to a boundary-
value problem in the air and a dispersion relation for the phase speed. Following [8, §2], we
derive the linearized equations by taking Eulerian variations along a one-parameter family of
solutions. These variations describe changes at fixed spatial points. Vertical advection of the
base-state density contributes a term involving ρ′
0 to the linearized mass conservation equation.
At the air–water interface, the pressure balance is evaluated at the displaced surface, so its
linearization also includes the change in base pressure caused by the interface displacement.
14


This change equals the base-state pressure gradient times the displacement, and adding it to
the Eulerian pressure perturbation gives the Lagrangian pressure variation on each side of the
interface.
By Hypothesis (H2), the base air and water velocities agree at the undisturbed interface.
The linearized kinematic conditions therefore require the vertical velocity perturbations on
both sides of the interface to agree. For a base state with an interfacial velocity jump, the
treatment of the velocity variations involves the Hodge projection described in [8, Remark 2.5],
which removes the gradient part of a vector field and keeps its divergence-free part. In the
present study, the water velocity perturbation must satisfy incompressibility, whereas the air
velocity perturbation has no such constraint.
We also seek to identify the growing normal modes obtained from the dispersion relation
with eigenfunctions of the linearized fluid equations. For this purpose, we introduce the operator
Lk, which expresses the time derivatives of the perturbation amplitudes at wavenumber k in
terms of those amplitudes. A normal mode with temporal factor e−ikct then corresponds to
an eigenfunction with eigenvalue λ = −ikc, and exponential growth corresponds to Re λ > 0.
Establishing this correspondence requires a precise choice of function spaces and operator
domain that incorporates the boundary conditions. Appendix C provides this construction
on fixed reference domains and, under the additional thermodynamic regularity stated there,
identifies the linearized equations with the Fr´echet derivative of the nonlinear formulation. The
theorem at the end of this section states the resulting correspondence between the dispersion
relation and the eigenvalues of Lk.
3.1
The linearized system
We derive the first-order equations for perturbations of the laminar state, including the
displacement of the air–water interface. We differentiate the governing equations in the air and
water at fixed spatial points. When differentiating the interface conditions, we also account for
the displacement of the air–water interface.
Let ϑ be a real perturbation parameter, and consider a family of solutions of Equations (8)–
(12) that are defined on a common time interval, whose air and water fields are twice continuously
differentiable as functions of (ϑ, t, x, z) up to the boundary of each phase, and whose interface
elevation η is three times continuously differentiable as a function of (ϑ, t, x). This regularity
allows us to exchange the derivative with respect to ϑ with the derivatives with respect to t, x,
and z, including the second derivative ∂2
xη in the curvature term of the dynamic condition. At
ϑ = 0, the solution is the laminar state in Equation (13). Differentiating with respect to ϑ at
ϑ = 0 gives the first-order perturbations of the interface and the fluid variables. We denote the
first variation of the interface elevation by
ζ := ∂ϑη|ϑ=0 .

In the air phase, the base velocity is (U(z), 0), so transport by the base wind produces the
operator ∂t + U∂x. The terms ρ′
0w and U′w arise from vertical advection of the base density
and the base wind velocity, respectively. The gravitational force contributes the term −gr
to the vertical momentum equation. The base entropy is constant, so its gradient adds no
term to the entropy equation. Together, these observations give Equation (34). In the water
phase, the base velocity is zero and the density is constant, so the same differentiation gives
Equation (35).

the water-side condition, this shows that the vertical air velocity perturbation w|z=0 and the
vertical water velocity perturbation v · ez|z=0 both equal the time derivative of the interface
displacement, which gives Equation (36). If U(0)̸ = 0, these two vertical velocity perturbations
instead differ by U(0)∂xζ, as in the vortex-sheet setting discussed in [8, §2].
We then differentiate the dynamic condition in Equation (11), which imposes the pressure
balance at the moving air–water interface. Because the base pressures vary with height, their
values at the interface change when the interface moves. The pressure variation on each side
is therefore the sum of the Eulerian pressure perturbation and the base pressure gradient
multiplied by ζ. Differentiating Equation (11) at ϑ = 0 yields

Remark 3.2. By instability at wavenumber k, we mean that the closed, densely defined operator
Lk of §3.3 has an eigenvalue λ with Re λ > 0. This notion of instability concerns only the
eigenvalues of Lk, as in Theorem 3.5. By Theorem C.6, these eigenvalues correspond exactly to
growing normal modes of the form given by Equation (44), with λ = −ikc and Re λ = k Im c > 0.
The spatial regularity of each mode depends on the regularity of the coefficients. Its temporal
factor e−ikct gives exponential growth at rate k Im c in every L2-based norm in which the
mode amplitude is finite. Here, for an interval I, L2(I) denotes the space of square-integrable
functions ψ on I, with the norm ∥ψ∥L2(I) :=
 R
I |ψ|2 dz
1/2. For an integer m ≥1, the Sobolev
space Hm(I) consists of the functions in L2(I) whose weak derivatives up to order m lie
in L2(I), with the norm ∥ψ∥Hm(I) :=
 Pm
i=0 ∥ψ(i)∥2
L2(I)
1/2. The weak derivative ψ′ is the
function, when it exists, that satisfies
R
I ψ′φ dz = −
R
I ψφ′ dz for every smooth function φ
that vanishes near the endpoints of I, and higher weak derivatives are defined by repeating
this step. A mode amplitude or a state lies in L2 or Hm when each of its component functions
does, where the air components are defined on [0, ha] and the water components on [−hw, 0].
The L2-based norms are the norms of these spaces. The analysis does not establish semigroup
generation, well-posedness of the initial-value problem, or nonlinear instability. Semigroup
generation would mean that the linear evolution in which the time derivative of the state is its
image under Lk has exactly one solution for every initial state in the domain of Lk, and that
the norm of each solution at time t is at most the norm of its initial state times an exponential
function of t that is the same for all initial states. Well-posedness means that solutions exist,
are unique, and depend continuously on the initial state. For well-posedness results in related
settings, see [15] for the viscous two-phase problem and [35, 51, 13, 40] for inviscid one-phase
compressible problems.

Definition 3.4. Let Im c > 0. By Lemma 5.1 below, the system in Equation (46) has a
unique solution with w(ha) = 0 and w′(ha) = 1. We denote it by w(·; k, c) and call it the
top-normalized solution. Its value w(0; k, c) at the interface is the interface trace. Here and
below, a dot marks the free argument and the arguments after a semicolon are parameters,
so w(·; k, c) is a function of the height z and w(0; k, ·) is a function of c. For the limiting
solution w∗(·; cr) of Proposition 5.7, the wavenumber k is omitted from the arguments. As in
Equation (49), ξ = w/(ikV ) is the vertical displacement amplitude of the air, and ξ(0) is the
interface displacement of the solution. Where the interface trace does not vanish, the air-side
pressure response is

Thus Za is the Lagrangian pressure perturbation at the interface, normalized by the surface
air density ερw and divided by the interface displacement. By Lemma 5.1, the top-normalized
solution spans the solutions of Equation (46) with w(ha) = 0, and it depends holomorphically
on c. The quotient in Equation (53) does not change when the solution is multiplied by a
nonzero constant, so Za does not depend on the choice of normalization. Since V (0) = −c̸ = 0,
the condition ξ(0)̸ = 0 is equivalent to w(0; k, c)̸ = 0. Where w(0; k, c)̸ = 0, we may therefore
rescale the solution so that ξ(0) = 1 as in Equation (49), and then Za(k, c) = ¯q(0) −¯ρ0(0)g.
The function Za depends only on the normalized profiles (¯ρ0, a, U) and not on ε (§2.4). By
Lemma 5.1, Za(k, ·) is meromorphic on the open upper half-plane, with poles exactly at the
zeros of w(0; k, ·). Here meromorphic means holomorphic except at isolated poles.

Multiplying the relation in Equation (54) by the interface trace w(0; k, c) gives the holomorphic
dispersion function E of Definition 4.8.
At ε = 0, the roots of Equation (54) are the roots ±ck of Dw(k, ·) given in Equation (24).
Since ∂cDw(k, ck) = 2kck coth(khw) > 0, the free wave speed ck is a simple root. To follow this
root for small ε > 0, we need Za(k, c) as the imaginary part of c tends to zero with c near ck.
Section 5 constructs the boundary value of Za(k, c), its limit as c = cr + iδ with δ ↓0, for real
cr near ck.
3.3
The linearized operator at one wavenumber
For each k, the operator Lk maps the spatial Fourier amplitudes at wavenumber k to their
time derivatives, after the water pressure has been recovered by solving Laplace’s equation
in the water, with its interface value given by the linearized dynamic condition and a zero
vertical derivative at the bottom. Its definition uses three notions. The linearized constraint
space is the set of states that satisfy the linearized constraints, namely incompressibility in the
water, the two kinematic conditions, the dynamic condition, and the conditions at the top and
bottom boundaries. The H3 core Dk consists of the states in this space with H3 regularity
whose water pressure solves Laplace’s equation in the water with a zero vertical derivative at
the bottom, and the linearized equations can be applied to these states directly. The graph of
an operator is the set of pairs formed by a state and its image. An operator is closed when
its graph contains every limit, in the norm of the L2-based state space, of a sequence of such
pairs. If adding to the graph all limits of such pairs in this norm gives again the graph of an
operator, that operator is the graph closure. It is the smallest closed operator that agrees with
the original operator on the domain of the original operator, and it is the standard way to
make a differential operator closed. The operator Lk is the graph closure of the linearized
evolution on the H3 core, so Dk is dense in the domain of Lk in the graph norm, the sum of
the L2 norms of a state and of its image under Lk.
Under the additional thermodynamic regularity in Equation (188), the flattened equations
of Section C.1, which are the equations transformed to fixed reference domains on which the
interface is flat, are Fr´echet differentiable. After the change of variables to the good unknown
in Equation (203), their derivative becomes the Eulerian linearized system.
Restricting
this evolution to perturbations proportional to eikx gives the spatial Fourier component at
wavenumber k. A normal mode adds the temporal factor e−ikct.
The theorem uses the following spectral notions for Lk, which acts on an L2 space of states
at wavenumber k (Definition C.4). A complex number λ belongs to the resolvent set of Lk
when, for every state F, the equation (λ −Lk)ˆU = F has exactly one solution ˆU in the domain
of Lk. The map F 7→ˆU is then the resolvent (λ −Lk)−1, and the spectrum of Lk is the set
of complex numbers that do not belong to the resolvent set. Every eigenvalue belongs to the
spectrum. An eigenvalue λ is an isolated point of the spectrum when some neighborhood of λ
contains no other point of the spectrum. For such an eigenvalue, the Riesz projection, also
called the spectral projection, is 1/(2πi) times the integral of the resolvent over a small circle
around λ, traversed counterclockwise (Corollary C.10). The eigenvalue is algebraically simple
when this projection has rank one, that is, a one-dimensional range. Then every solution of
(Lk −λ)m ˆU = 0 with an integer m ≥1 is a multiple of one eigenvector. Here (Lk −λ)m ˆU is
defined when ˆU and its images under (Lk −λ)j for j = 1, . . . , m −1 lie in the domain of Lk.
The following theorem states the construction and its spectral consequences. Its proof is given
in Appendix C.
Theorem 3.5. Assume Hypotheses (H1) and (H2). For parts (i) and (ii), assume in addition
the thermodynamic regularity in Equation (188), which requires an open set U whose closure U
is a compact subset of (0, ∞) × R and which contains the base range {(ρ0(z), S∗) : z ∈[0, ha]},
with P ∈C5(U ) and infU Pρ > 0. Here P ∈C5(U ) means that the partial derivatives of P of
20


order at most five are continuous on U and extend continuously to U . Then the following
statements hold.
(i) In the flattened formulation of Section C.1, the two-phase residual, whose components are
the right-hand sides of the flattened evolution equations and the expressions set to zero in
the flattened constraint equations, is continuously Fr´echet differentiable, that is, with a
derivative that depends continuously on the state, on an open neighborhood of the laminar
state. After the change of variables to the good unknown in Equation (203), its derivative
at the laminar state is the linearized system of Proposition 3.1 (Propositions C.2 and C.3).
(ii) For each k > 0, take the part of the linearized evolution in (i), written in terms of
the good unknown, that is proportional to eikx. Use pressure rather than density as the
thermodynamic state variable, recover the water pressure by solving Laplace’s equation in
the water, and restrict to the linearized constraint space. The resulting evolution defines
the closed, densely defined operator Lk of Definition C.4, which acts on L2. Precisely,
Lk is the graph closure of this evolution on the H3 core Dk in the constraint space
(Proposition C.7).
(iii) The operator Lk itself is defined under Hypotheses (H1) and (H2) alone, without the
thermodynamic regularity in Equation (188). For Im c̸ = 0, λ = −ikc is an eigenvalue of
Lk if and only if the holomorphic dispersion function E(k, c, ε) of Definition 4.8 vanishes,
and the eigenspace is then one-dimensional (Theorem C.6). At a simple zero of E(k, ·, ε),
the eigenvalue is an isolated point of the spectrum of Lk and is algebraically simple. In
this case, the resolvent satisfies an explicit bound and the Riesz projection has rank one
(Proposition C.9 and Corollary C.10).
4
Equivalent forms of the air equations and the holomorphic
dispersion function
Unless stated otherwise, we assume Im c̸ = 0 in this section, so V = U −c is nonzero on
[0, ha]. We derive three equivalent forms of the air equations. The displacement–pressure
system exposes the role of neutrality, the vertical-velocity equation identifies the critical-layer
coefficient, and the mass-weighted displacement equation yields the flux and energy identities.
We then construct a holomorphic dispersion function.
4.1
Isentropic reduction and the displacement–pressure system
The entropy equation first reduces the air system to displacement and pressure. This reduction
is isentropic, that is, the entropy perturbation of a normal mode vanishes (Lemma 4.1). We
write S0 for the entropy of the base state, so S0 ≡S∗and S′
0 ≡0 for the laminar states of §2.3.
We use the vertical Lagrangian displacement amplitude ξ = w/(ikV ) of Proposition 3.3, so
that w = ikV ξ. This relation expresses the vertical velocity as the material derivative of the
displacement along the base wind. This material derivative is the time derivative following the
base wind, ∂t + U∂x, and it acts on a normal mode as multiplication by ik(U −c) = ikV . The
next lemma shows that the Lagrangian pressure and density variations satisfy a barotropic
relation, that is, a relation that does not involve the entropy.

buoyancy effects, leaving the critical singularity as the object of the analysis.

k2(V 2/ρ0)X, as required. The stated incompressible limits follow directly as a →∞.
We use Equation (61) for w and its first-order companion, Equation (60), for the critical-layer
construction in §5. Equation (62) for X instead yields the integral formula in Equation (69) for
Im Za, the coercivity estimate of Lemma 6.1, and the uniform-wind example of §8.1. Although
both equations are formally self-adjoint for real c away from critical and sonic levels, they
depend nonlinearly on the phase speed c through V , A, and F.
At a regular critical level, the leading coefficient in Equation (62) vanishes quadratically,
F ∼U′(zc)2(z −zc)2/ρ0(zc), where ∼means that the ratio of the two sides tends to 1 as z →zc.
The Frobenius exponents, that is, the powers s for which the equation has a solution that
behaves like (z −zc)s near zc, are 0, −1 for X and 1, 0 for w. Since these exponents differ by
an integer, the second solution may contain a logarithmic term. The next proposition identifies
its coefficient. The construction in §5 also covers the case in which this coefficient vanishes.

base-state density gradient. In meteorological variables, the relevant quantity is the specific
vorticity U′/ρ0, whose critical-level gradient controls momentum deposition in compressible
wave–mean-flow theory [19, 3, 7]. With the opposite curl convention, the specific vorticity is
−U′/ρ0, but Q and the instability criterion remain unchanged.

4.3
The wave-momentum flux and the imaginary part of Za
The sign of the growth rate is determined by the air-side pressure response. We relate its
imaginary part to a flux that is conserved between critical levels. First normalize by the
surface air density, setting ¯ρ0 = ρ0/(ερw), ¯A = A/(ερw), ¯χ = χ/(ερw), and ¯q = q/(ερw).
The quantities A, χ, q, and G scale linearly in the density, whereas F scales inversely. Thus
Equations (60), (61), and (62) have the same form with ρ0 replaced by ¯ρ0. We henceforth
use normalized quantities, with the same symbols G, F, and X for their normalized versions.
In particular, X = ¯ρ0 ξ, X(0) = ξ(0), and G(zc) = ¯ρ0(zc)Q(zc). An overbar always denotes
normalization, and complex conjugation is written with an asterisk, as in w∗.

4.4
A holomorphic form of the dispersion relation
The quotient dispersion relation Dw(k, c) = εZa(k, c) is convenient near the water-wave root,
provided the interface trace is nonzero. For the spectral characterization, we also need a
holomorphic function defined at the poles of Za. We obtain it by multiplying the dispersion
relation by the interface trace w(0; k, c).
For Im c̸ = 0, let (w, ¯χ)(·; k, c) solve Equation (60) with
w(ha; k, c) = 0,
¯χ(ha; k, c) = ¯A(ha; c) V (ha; c).
(73)
The first equation of the system in Equation (60) gives w′(ha) = 1, so w satisfies the normal-
ization of Definition 3.4. The interface trace w(0; k, c) vanishes precisely when the air equation
has a nontrivial solution with w(0) = w(ha) = 0.
When w(0; k, c)̸ = 0, division by w(0; k, c)/(ikV (0)) imposes the interface normalization
ξ(0) = 1 in Equation (53). For real cr, we will also use the relative velocity profile ˆy = w∗/w∗(0),
where w∗is the limiting solution of Proposition 5.7. Clearing the interface trace from the
quotient dispersion relation gives the following function.

Lemma 4.9. Let k > 0 and ε > 0. Then the following hold.
(i) E(k, ·, ε) is holomorphic on the open upper half-plane and on the open lower half-plane,
with the reflection symmetry E(k, c∗, ε) = E(k, c, ε)∗.
(ii) For Im c̸ = 0, the linearized problem has a nontrivial normal mode of the form given by
Equation (44) at (k, c) if and only if E(k, c, ε) = 0.
(iii) If E(k, c, ε) = 0 with Im c̸ = 0, then w(0; k, c)̸ = 0. Wherever w(0; k, c)̸ = 0,
E(k, c, ε) = w(0; k, c)

(iv) E(k, c, 0) = Dw(k, c) w(0; k, c).
Proof. Holomorphy in (i) follows from Lemma 5.1. For the reflection symmetry, the coefficient
matrix M(z; c) of Equation (60) and the values at z = ha in Equation (73) are rational in
c with real z-dependent coefficients, so M(z; c∗) = M(z; c)∗, and likewise for these values.
Hence z 7→(w, ¯χ)(z; k, c)∗solves the system at c∗and satisfies Equation (73) at c∗, and
uniqueness gives (w, ¯χ)(z; k, c∗) = (w, ¯χ)(z; k, c)∗. Finally Dw(k, c∗) = Dw(k, c)∗since Dw is a
real polynomial in c.
We next prove (iii), which is needed for the mode correspondence. Suppose w(0; k, c) = 0.
Then E(k, c, ε) = −εV (0)¯χ(0; k, c). Since V (0) = −c̸ = 0 and ε > 0, the equality E = 0 would
imply ¯χ(0; k, c) = 0. Uniqueness for the regular system with (w, ¯χ)(0) = (0, 0) would then
force (w, ¯χ) ≡0. This contradicts Equation (73), whose second component ¯A(ha)V (ha) is
nonzero for Im c̸ = 0 by Lemma 5.1. Substitution of Equation (78) into Equation (74) gives the
factorization in Equation (75). Since w(0; k, ·) is nonzero on the open set under consideration,
the two dispersion functions have the same orders of vanishing there. Part (iv) follows by
setting ε = 0 in Equation (74).
To prove (ii), let a nontrivial normal mode be given. By Proposition 3.3, it has ˆζ̸ = 0 and
w(0) = ikV (0)ˆζ̸ = 0, since c̸ = 0. Its air component solves Equation (60) with w(ha) = 0. This
solution space is one-dimensional by Lemma 5.1, so the air component is a nonzero multiple
µ (w, ¯χ)(·; k, c) of the top-normalized solution.
The dynamic condition in Equation (50),
written for general ˆζ in normalized variables, is Dwˆζ = ε
 ¯q(0) −gˆζ

with ¯ρ0(0) = 1. Inserting
ˆζ = µ w(0; k, c)/(ikV (0)) and ¯q(0) = µ ¯χ(0; k, c)/(ik) and multiplying by ikV (0)/µ gives
E(k, c, ε) = 0.
Conversely, if E(k, c, ε) = 0 then w(0; k, c)̸ = 0 by (iii). Set ˆζ := w(0; k, c)/(ikV (0))̸ = 0
and reconstruct the water fields by Equation (48). Then the two kinematic conditions and the
dynamic condition hold by construction, and Proposition 3.3 produces the mode.
Remark 4.10. The zeros of the interface trace and of the dispersion function have different
meanings. A zero of w(0; k, ·) is a pole of Za(k, ·), because ¯χ(0; k, c)̸ = 0 there by uniqueness
of the top-normalized solution and the quotient in Equation (78) diverges. For Im c̸ = 0, a
zero of E instead corresponds to a normal mode and cannot be a zero of the trace, by (iii).
Proposition 3.3 excludes the possibility of a decoupled air mode with ˆζ = 0 for k > 0 and
Im c̸ = 0.
At ε = 0, the factorization (iv) explains the non-resonance assumption in Hypothesis (H5).
If this assumption fails at cr = ck, the free wave speed ck is a zero of the first factor Dw(k, ·)
and also of the limit w∗(0; ·) of the second factor as the imaginary part of c tends to zero.
The proof of Theorem 6.4 uses Dw(k, c) −εZa(k, c) = 0, and therefore requires division by
the interface trace. It does not apply at such a common zero. Near such a common zero, the
function E is still holomorphic for Im c̸ = 0, but we do not study its behavior at real phase
speeds and leave the resonant case open. Under Hypothesis (H5), the roots of D near ck are
equivalently the zeros of E.
5
Limiting absorption and the residue formula for the pressure
response
In this section, we construct the boundary value of the air-side pressure response Za(k, c) as
the positive imaginary part of the complex phase speed c tends to zero. We first analyze the
vertical-velocity equation near each critical level, where the limiting solution has a logarithmic
term with coefficient Q(zj)/U′(zj), and then combine these local solutions over the whole air
layer. The main result, Theorem 5.8, shows that the boundary value Z+
a (k, cr) exists with
a rate and depends C1 on cr, and that its imaginary part is a sum of contributions from
27


the critical levels, each proportional to Q(zj). We also extend Za continuously to a closed
neighborhood of ck in the closed upper half-plane (Proposition 5.11) and show that the limiting
solution does not vanish at the highest critical level (Lemma 5.13). These results are used in
§6 to prove the main instability theorem.

5.1
The pressure response for complex phase speeds
For complex phase speeds with Im c̸ = 0, the equation has no singularity, and the top-normalized
solution depends holomorphically on the phase speed. The next lemma records this and the
resulting form of Za.
Lemma 5.1. For Im c̸ = 0 the coefficients of Equation (77) are continuous on [0, ha] and
holomorphic in c, because V has no zero, and neither does a2−V 2, since Im(V 2) = 2 Re V Im V
can vanish only where Re V = 0, and there a2 −V 2 = a2 + (Im c)2 > 0. No subsonicity
assumption is needed in this case. The top-normalized solution (w, ¯χ)(·; k, c) of Section 4.4,
with the values at z = ha given in Equation (73), exists and is unique. This solution spans
the solution space of Equation (77) subject to w(ha) = 0 and is holomorphic in c on each
open half-plane. Consequently Za(k, ·) is meromorphic on the open upper half-plane, with poles
exactly at the zeros of w(0; k, ·). Moreover,

Proof. For Im c = δ̸ = 0 we have |V | ≥|δ| on [0, ha], and a2 −V 2̸ = 0 as shown in the
statement. Hence the coefficients of Equation (77) are continuous in z and holomorphic in
c, and the solvability and analyticity claims follow from the standard theory of linear ODEs
with parameters [11, Ch. 1]. The solution space with w(ha) = 0 is one-dimensional, because a
solution is determined uniquely by its values (w, ¯χ)(ha), which can be prescribed arbitrarily in
C2, so the solutions with w(ha) = 0 are the multiples of the solution with (w, ¯χ)(ha) = (0, 1).
If w(0; k, c) = 0 then ¯χ(0; k, c)̸ = 0, since otherwise (w, ¯χ) ≡0 by uniqueness, contradicting
Equation (73). Thus every zero of w(0; k, ·) is a pole of Za. Equation (78) restates Equation (53)
using ¯q = ¯χ/(ik), ξ(0) = w(0)/(ikV (0)).
5.2
Logarithmic solutions and the flux jump at a critical level
In this subsection, we study the solutions of Equation (77) on a small interval around one
critical level zj. For δ > 0 the equation is regular there, but the limit of its solutions as δ ↓0
contains a logarithmic term at zj. To control this limit, we rewrite the system as an integral
equation in which the logarithm appears explicitly, and we solve it by a contraction argument
with bounds that hold uniformly for δ ∈[0, δ1]. At δ = 0, this gives two basis solutions, a
regular one that vanishes at zj and one that contains the logarithmic term with coefficient
Q(zj)/U′(zj) (Proposition 5.4). By Equation (99), the logarithmic term produces a jump of
the Wronskian-type flux W across zj, proportional to Q(zj) |w(zj)|2. Proposition 5.6 then
shows that the solutions for δ > 0 converge to these limits with an explicit rate.
Consider a critical level zj and an interval Ij = [zj −ς, zj + ς] ⊂(0, ha) containing no other
critical level, with mU := minIj |U′| > 0. Choose ς small from the profiles, as the results below
require, and impose δ1 ≤mUς. Set the reference speed V∗= mUς. Every logarithm below
is made dimensionless with this scale, and O(δ log(1/δ)) abbreviates O

Remark 5.5. Equation (96) shows that the compressible critical layer has the same type of
singularity as Rayleigh’s equation, with U′′ replaced by Q. For the zero-gravity, temperature-
stratified gas the same computation, with the unified coefficient of Remark 4.5, reproduces
the classical local expansions of compressible boundary-layer theory [41, Eqs. (9.13)–(9.14)],
including the −iπ branch rule. The derivation makes the physical origin of the two pieces of
Q = U′′ + bU′ transparent. The curvature U′′ enters through the curvature of the profile in
the variation-of-constants kernel, and bU′ through the hydrostatic decay ¯χ′ = −b¯χ + O(V ) of
the scaled pressure variable across the layer.

5.3
The limiting solution and the boundary value of Za
We now assemble the local solutions across all critical levels into a global limit and identify the

Proposition 5.7. Let cr be a regular value as above and define the limit problem on [0, ha] as
the system in Equation (77) with δ = 0 on [0, ha]\{z1, . . . , zm}, continued across each zj by the
representation in Equation (84), equivalently by the basis of Proposition 5.4. Then the solution
(w, ¯χ)(·; k, cr + iδ) with values (0, ¯Aδ(ha)Vδ(ha)) at z = ha converges as δ ↓0, uniformly on

and C1 away from the critical levels, and at each zj its derivative has the logarithmic behavior
and the jump in the renormalized derivative in Equations (97)–(98) of Proposition 5.4(ii). The
function ¯χ∗is Lipschitz on [0, ha], and (w∗, ¯χ∗)(0), w∗(zj) depend C1 on cr.
Proof. Partition [0, ha] into the local intervals Ij and the closed intervals between them that
contain no critical level. On the latter intervals the coefficients of Equation (77) are C2
uniformly in δ ∈[0, δ1] and Lipschitz in δ, so the solution maps are invertible linear maps that
converge at rate O(δ) by Gr¨onwall’s inequality and depend C1 on cr. The values at the top
boundary also depend on δ, and Yδ(ha) := (0, ¯Aδ(ha)Vδ(ha)) satisfies Yδ(ha) −Y0(ha) = O(δ)
by uniform subsonicity. Since the solution is obtained from Yδ(ha) by composing finitely
many uniformly bounded maps, as described below, this O(δ) difference does not change
the stated rate. Across each Ij, Lemma 5.3 identifies the solution with a fixed point, whose
constants (α, β) depend linearly and invertibly on the values at zj + ς, with bounds uniform in
δ by Proposition 5.6(ii). The same proposition gives convergence of Tδ at rate O(δ log(1/δ)),
together with C1-dependence on cr. Composing the finitely many maps from ha down to 0
proves the claims. The interior description of w∗is Proposition 5.4(i)–(ii).

of critical-level contributions, each proportional to Q(zj)|w∗(zj)|2/|U′(zj)|.

Remark 5.9. Equation (109) gives the signed change of wave-momentum flux across each critical
level. When the sign condition for Miles growth holds, the flux is absorbed at the critical levels
in proportion to the specific-vorticity gradient. Equation (110) shows that the air-pressure
component in phase with the interface slope, equivalently shifted by a quarter wavelength from
the interface displacement, is determined by the total critical-layer flux change. This is the
rigorous, compressible form of Miles’s resonance argument [45, 34] and of the Eliassen–Palm
theory, in which steady waves without dissipation leave the mean flow unchanged except where
their momentum flux is absorbed, as at critical levels [19, 6, 3]. For N2 = 0 there is no
internal-gravity-wave radiation, so the flux changes only across the critical levels and vanishes
above the highest one because w(ha) = 0.
Remark 5.10. The map cr 7→w∗(0; cr) is C1 on intervals of regular values with the same
number of critical levels (Proposition 5.7). Hypothesis (H5) requires this trace to be nonzero.
It follows from Lemma 5.13 under the sign condition of Corollary 6.7, and it can otherwise be
checked for a prescribed profile. A zero of w∗(0; cr) means that the atmospheric boundary-value
problem with the interface held at rest has a nontrivial solution at cr. When the equation
for w has solutions that oscillate in height, such zeros include resonances of the air layer
between the interface held at rest and the top boundary, for example acoustic standing waves
when the flow relative to the wave is supersonic. In the uniform-wind example of §8.1, the
corresponding solutions in the uniformly subsonic regime are evanescent, that is, they do not
oscillate in height, and they decay exponentially with height. For a general wind profile, such
solutions need not decay monotonically or exponentially with height. Such a zero is a property
of the limiting air problem alone. Proposition 3.3 excludes coupled normal modes with ˆζ = 0
only for Im c̸ = 0, and we do not apply it at the real phase speed cr. At cr = ck, such a
zero means that ck is a zero of both Dw(k, ·) and w∗(0; ·), the limit of the second factor of
E(k, c, 0) = Dw(k, c) w(0; k, c), as described in Remark 4.10.
The proof of the instability theorem in §6 requires Za on the set D+
r0 defined below, a
closed neighborhood of ck in the closed upper half-plane. Its boundary values at real phase
speeds alone do not suffice. The next proposition supplies the extension and the quantitative
non-resonance bound.

[0, zm). In particular u1(0) > 0, i.e. Hypothesis (H5) holds automatically, and W(0)̸ = 0.
Proof. Consider the limiting solution at the real phase speed cr.

6
The compressible Miles instability
In this section, we prove the instability theorem. We first show that without a critical layer
the root of the dispersion relation near ck stays real, so that the corresponding mode is neutral
(Theorem 6.2). This isolates the critical layer as the only source of growth near ck. With a
critical layer, we compare the dispersion function with its linearization at ck on a neighborhood
of ck in the upper half-plane. A boundary version of Rouch´e’s theorem (Lemma 6.3), applied
with the boundary value of Za from Theorem 5.8, yields the unstable root, its expansion, and
its growth rate (Theorem 6.4). The positivity result of Lemma 5.13 then turns the residue
formula in Equation (110) into the sign criterion (Corollaries 6.7 and 6.8).
6.1
Neutrality and persistence without a critical layer
Assume that ck lies outside the range of U. Then Equation (62), the self-adjoint form of
the displacement equation, is coercive near ck. Coercive means that its quadratic form is
bounded below by positive multiples of the L2 norms of X′ and X (Lemma 6.1). This excludes
zeros of the interface trace w(0; k, c) and gives | Im Za(k, c)| ≤C| Im c|. Consequently the root
of the dispersion relation near ck remains real and simple. This extends [8, Lemma 3.1] to
compressible air.

6.2
The unstable root and the sign criterion
The unstable root is found by comparing the dispersion function with its linearization at ck on
a small set in the upper half-plane. Because the boundary values of Za at real phase speeds
enter, we need Rouch´e’s theorem for a region whose boundary contains a real interval, on which
the functions are only continuous. We use the following boundary version of Rouch´e’s theorem.
Lemma 6.3. Let c0 ∈R, r > 0, and D+ := {c ∈C : |c −c0| < r, Im c > 0}, with closure D+
and boundary ∂D+. Let φ, ψ be holomorphic in D+ and continuous on D+, with |φ −ψ| < |ψ|
on ∂D+. Then φ and ψ have finitely many zeros in D+ and equally many, counted with
multiplicity, and neither vanishes on ∂D+.
Proof. Set m := min∂D+
 |ψ| −|φ −ψ|

> 0. Since φ and ψ are uniformly continuous on the
compact set D+, they have a common modulus of continuity ω0, that is, a nondecreasing function
on [0, ∞) with ω0(t) →0 as t ↓0 such that |φ(c) −φ(c′)| ≤ω0(|c −c′|) and |ψ(c) −ψ(c′)| ≤
ω0(|c −c′|) for all c, c′ ∈D+. For t ∈(0, r/2) let Dt := {c : |c −c0| < r −t, Im c > t}, a
convex set with Dt ⊂D+ whose boundary is a piecewise-smooth simple closed curve. Every
c ∈D+ \ Dt lies within distance t of ∂D+. If Im c ≤t, its vertical projection to the real
diameter lies in ∂D+ at distance ≤t. If |c −c0| ≥r −t, its radial projection to the semicircle
43


does, because the projection preserves the sign of the imaginary part. Hence, for such c with
nearest boundary point c′,
|ψ(c)| ≥|ψ(c′)| −ω0(t) ≥m + |φ −ψ|(c′) −ω0(t),
|φ −ψ|(c) ≤|φ −ψ|(c′) + 2ω0(t), (127)
so that |φ −ψ|(c) < |ψ(c)| and |φ(c)| ≥|ψ(c)| −|φ −ψ|(c) ≥m −3ω0(t) > 0 whenever
3ω0(t) < m. Choose such a t. Then φ and ψ have no zeros in D+ \ Dt, in particular none
on ∂D+, the strict inequality |φ −ψ| < |ψ| holds on ∂Dt, and φ, ψ are holomorphic on a
neighborhood of Dt ⊂D+. Rouch´e’s theorem on Dt, whose boundary is a simple closed curve,
gives equal, finite zero counts there, which are the zero counts in D+.

Remark 6.9. Here ck > 0 denotes the right-going root of Dw given by Equation (24). The
left-going root −ck is covered by the reflection (x, U) 7→(−x, −U). The same argument then
applies to the reflected problem, whose critical levels are the solutions of U = −ck, with the
sign condition in Equation (138) read with ck replaced by −ck. The two roots have independent
sets of critical levels. For the non-negative winds of §8 the left-going wave has no critical level,
and Theorem 6.2, applied after reflection, keeps it neutral.
Remark 6.10. Since g/a2 > 0, for a wind profile that increases with height with shear decreasing
upward (U′ > 0 > U′′) compressibility shifts the local coefficient Q = U′′ + (g/a2)U′ upward.
While Q(zc) < 0 this reduces |Q(zc)| below |U′′(zc)|, and the criterion in Equation (139) fails
altogether once
g
a2(zc) ≥|U′′(zc)|
U′(zc) .

For a logarithmic profile U = (u∗/κK) log(1+z/z0), where u∗is the friction velocity, κK the von
K´arm´an constant, and z0 the roughness length, the condition in Equation (140) holds beyond
the height at which zc + z0 = a2/g. For the bounded shear-layer profile U = U∞(1 −e−z/L) of
limiting speed U∞and shear depth L, it holds uniformly in z when a2 < gL. In the second
case, consider a wavenumber that satisfies the assumptions of Proposition 8.2 and lies in the
incompressible Miles band, which is the set of wavenumbers k for which ck lies in the range of U
with U′′ < 0 at the critical levels. At such a wavenumber the sum in Equation (131) is positive,
so the leading Miles coefficient Im c1 changes sign and the local O(ε) Miles bifurcation from ck
is absent (Proposition 8.2). At the same time the growth rate is not a function of Q(zc) alone.
The amplitude |ˆy(zc)|2 and the weight ¯ρ0(zc) are themselves modified by compressibility, and
the coefficient ¯A/¯ρ0 = a2/(a2 −V 2) also changes the vertical boundary-value problem. Hence
the sign of the net correction is not decided by the local criterion alone. For the parameters
of wind waves over the sea, the compressible corrections are at most about 10−3 locally and
10−2 globally (§8.2, Remark 7.4), and the condition in Equation (140) would require critical
layers 8.4 km above the sea surface. Miles’s incompressible criterion is therefore insensitive to
compressibility for water waves, and Theorem 7.1 makes this precise at each wavenumber.
Remark 6.11. As in [8, Rem. 4.3], Corollary 6.7 applies for every k such that ck lies in the
range of U with Q < 0 at the critical levels. With surface tension the set of such k is a bounded
band, because large-k waves are too fast, with ck →∞. Its edges are set by ck ∈U([0, ha]) as
in the incompressible theory, except near the compressible cutoff in Equation (140), where the
band is further truncated. The Kelvin–Helmholtz mechanism is absent by Hypothesis (H2).
The compressible Kelvin–Helmholtz problem for uniform wind is treated in closed form in §8.1
for comparison.

Remark 7.4. The scaling in Equation (141) sends the acoustic ratio |V |2/a2 and the hydrostatic
ratio gzc/a2 to zero at order M2. The former enters through ¯A = ¯ρ0a2/(a2 −V 2), while the
latter enters through ¯ρ0 and Q −U′′ = (g/a2)U′. For ocean–atmosphere parameters, the global
Mach parameter satisfies M2 ≲10−2 for the wavenumbers of wind waves. Here ≲means that
the left side is at most of the order of the right side. The squared local wave Mach number
(ck/a(zc))2 and the hydrostatic ratio gzc/a(zc)2 are each typically at most 10−3.

8
Uniform, logarithmic, and shear-layer winds
In this section, we use examples to explain the results physically and to relate them to winds over
the sea. A uniform wind forms a vortex sheet at the interface, as in the classical compressible
Kelvin–Helmholtz problem, so it lies outside Hypothesis (H2) and the results of §6 and §7 do
not apply to it. We include it for comparison, because its air-side pressure response has a
closed form (Proposition 8.1). The closed form shows how the sound speed and the density
scale height of the air set the rate at which the perturbation decays with height, so in this
example an upper boundary several decay lengths above the interface has little effect. For the
logarithmic wind of the neutral surface layer over the sea and for a bounded shear layer, we
evaluate the critical-layer coefficient Q (§8.2). For the logarithmic wind, the compressible term
(g/a2)U′ in Q is at most about 10−3|U′′| at critical levels within about 10 m of the sea surface,
so the criterion in Equation (139) agrees with Miles’s criterion U′′(zc) < 0 for winds over the
sea. We then consider the shear-layer wind in a model atmosphere with constant sound speed
below the gravity speed √gL of the shear layer. In this model, Q > 0 everywhere although
U′′ < 0, and the Miles bifurcation is absent (Proposition 8.2). This regime does not occur over
the sea, but it shows that the sign criterion of the incompressible theory does not extend to a
compressible atmosphere in general.
8.1
Closed-form pressure response for a uniform wind
For comparison with the classical compressible Kelvin–Helmholtz problem [46, 43], we solve
the air equation for a uniform wind in the constant-sound-speed atmosphere of Example 2.2.
This case lies outside Hypothesis (H2) and provides an exactly solvable subsonic example. In
this atmosphere the sound speed is constant, a ≡ae, and its value is a free parameter, which
need not be the value for air.
Proposition 8.1. Let the atmosphere have constant sound speed a and density ¯ρ0 = e−bz

Uniform wind gives U(0) = U0̸ = 0 and hence a vortex sheet, so the coupled reduction of
Proposition 3.3 does not apply. The kinematic conditions give the interface values wa(0) =
ik(U0 −c)ˆζ and ww(0) = −ikcˆζ of the vertical velocity in the air and in the water. Nevertheless,
the air-side displacement remains ξ(0) = w(0)/(ikV (0)) = ˆζ.

a
(k, c), which has the form of Equation (54) and is the compressible Kelvin–

velocity jumps across the interface. The operator Lk of Theorem 3.5 and the results of §6 and
§7 require this velocity to be continuous, which Hypothesis (H2) ensures, so we use this wind
only for comparison.
The acoustic factor (1 −V 2/a2) and the scale-height shift −b/2 both enter the vertical
decay exponent. Their combined effect is the full expression in Equation (153). The term −b/2
is the Lamb correction associated with the density scale height [20]. Up to a nonzero factor,
1 + Θ = 1 −e−2νha is the interface trace w(0; k, c) from Section 4.4. For real subsonic c it is
positive, so Hypothesis (H5) holds in this uniform-wind example and the vertical mode decays
exponentially with height.
Since |Θ| = e−2 Re ν ha, the finite-height correction in Equation (153) is O(e−2 Re ν ha). If
Re(1 −V 2/a2) ≥δ0, the principal branch satisfies Re ν ≥k√δ0 because Re √ζ ≥√Re ζ for
Re ζ > 0. For the ocean–atmosphere scales used above, |V |2/a2 ≲10−2, so the subsonic
factor is at least about 0.99. A top boundary several decay lengths 1/ Re ν above the interface
therefore has little effect in this example. No estimate for removing the top boundary is proved
here for variable shear.
