8.3
Failure of the Miles bifurcation under strong compressibility
For the shear-layer wind, −U′′ = U′/L, so in the constant-sound-speed model the condition
a2 < gL means that the hydrostatic term (g/a2)U′ in Q dominates the curvature term U′′
at every height. This reverses the sign of the critical-layer residue and of the leading Miles
coefficient. The sign condition is independent of k, although the threshold ε0 in Theorem 6.4
may depend on k.

Remark 8.3. Proposition 8.2 excludes an unstable root near ck but does not produce a decaying
eigenvalue. Because the coefficients are real, complex phase speeds with Im c̸ = 0 occur
in conjugate pairs (Lemma 4.9(i)). An isolated lower-half-plane partner would require the
excluded upper-half-plane root. The negative value Im Z+
a (k, ck) is the signed pressure work
on a prescribed neutral wave, not an eigenvalue decay rate.
A
Thermodynamic identities and the general stratified system
In this appendix, we derive the normal-mode equations for a base state whose entropy varies
with height, to show where the neutrality assumption of the main text enters. In the main text,
the entropy is constant and the Brunt–V¨ais¨al¨a frequency vanishes (Equation (15)). Neutrality
enters in two places. First, it removes the buoyancy term N2 from these equations, so the
equation near a critical level is of Rayleigh type and not of Taylor–Goldstein type (Remark 4.6).
Second, it gives the density gradient ρ′
0/ρ0 = −g/a2, and with this gradient the critical-layer
coefficient is Q = U′′ + (g/a2)U′ of Equation (64). Remark 4.5 compares Q with the coefficient

B
Technical estimates for limiting absorption and the low-Mach
limit
In this appendix, we prove the estimates that were deferred from §5 and §7. As in §5, C
denotes a positive constant that may change from line to line. The first three subsections
complete the local analysis near a critical level in §5.2. Section B.1 proves that a solution
of the integral representation of Lemma 5.2 solves the differential system, §B.2 proves the
logarithmic decomposition of the singular integral, and §B.3 compares this decomposition for
δ > 0 and δ = 0, which gives the convergence rate in Proposition 5.6. Section B.4 shows that
the local solutions depend Lipschitz-continuously on the coefficients of the equations, which
the low-Mach limit of §7 requires. Section B.5 proves that the local solutions depend C1 on
the real part cr of the phase speed, as stated in Propositions 5.6 and 5.7. Section B.6 combines
these results into the statements uniform in the Mach number that the proof of Theorem 7.1
uses.
B.1
Equivalence of the integral representation
In this subsection, we verify in detail the converse part of Lemma 5.2, which states that a
pair satisfying the integral representation in Equation (84) solves the system in Equation (77)
wherever V̸ = 0. By this converse, the fixed points of Lemma 5.3 solve the differential system,
also at δ = 0 away from the critical level.

Equation (84b) and use (V E)′ = (V ′/V + b) V E, which follows from E′ = bE, we obtain

B.2
Logarithmic decomposition of the singular integral
In this subsection, we prove the decomposition in Equation (88) and the regularity of its

part integrates to an explicit logarithm, and the difference vanishes at the critical level, so its
quotient by τ −iδ is bounded.

B.4
A coefficient-perturbation lemma for the local solutions
In this subsection, we show that the local solutions and the maps built from them depend
Lipschitz-continuously on the coefficient functions of the representation in Equation (84)
(Lemma B.1). The low-Mach limit of §7 needs this estimate, because there the coefficients
depend on the Mach number and we compare the local solutions of two atmospheres. We
therefore regard the coefficient functions as independent functions.
Let Ij and δ ∈[0, δ1] be given. We consider tuples a = (b, ¯ρ0, f, g1, g2) of functions on Ij
that share U and c, and hence V , τ, the base point zj, and the branch of the logarithm. We
assume that each tuple satisfies the bounds of §5.2 with a common constant, that ς is small
in terms of this constant, as in §5.2, and that the map B of Lemma 5.3, built from the tuple,
is a 1

factor 1

obeys the same bounds uniformly and depends on δ as in Equation (107). In §5.2, the tuple is
computed from one atmosphere, with f = 1/( ¯AE) and with g1 and g2 given by Equation (82).
We say that a tuple satisfies the first-order-system identities when g1 and g2 are obtained from
b, ¯ρ0, and f by Equation (82), that is, g1 = (f/U′)′ −bf/U′ and g2 = ¯ρ0k2f/U′. For such a
tuple, we compute E from b as in §5.2 and set ¯A = 1/(fE). Then, by §B.1, every solution
of the system in Equation (84) solves the system in Equation (77) with coefficients ( ¯A, b, ¯ρ0)

B.5
Parameter dependence in moving coordinates
In this subsection, we prove that the local solutions depend C1 on the real part cr of the phase
speed, as stated in Propositions 5.6 and 5.7. Two features of the original coordinates prevent
direct differentiation. The base point zj(cr) and the interval Ij(cr) = [zj(cr) −ς, zj(cr) + ς]
move with cr, and the derivative ∂cr[τ Log(τ −i0)] at fixed z is singular at the critical level.
We therefore pull each critical neighborhood back to a fixed interval, on which neither the
base point nor the endpoints move. In the new variable, the derivative of τ with respect to cr
vanishes at the critical level, and this factor cancels the logarithmic singularity.
Lemma B.2. Let K be a compact set of regular values of U with the same number of critical
levels, and pull each critical neighborhood back to the common interval [−ς, ς] by z = zj(cr) + x.
Assume that the coefficient tuple a = (b, ¯ρ0, f, g1, g2) is, in the pulled-back variables, C1 in cr
with values in C1([−ς, ς]), and that it ranges in a bounded set on which the map B of Lemma 5.3
is a 1
2-contraction. Then the following hold uniformly for δ ∈[0, δ1] and cr ∈K.
(i) The pulled-back map B is C1 in cr as a map into C0([−ς, ς]), with uniformly bounded
derivative.
58


(ii) The pulled-back fixed points, as maps into C0([−ς, ς]), the endpoint evaluation matrices
E±, and the critical values w(zj(cr)) are C1 in cr with uniformly bounded derivatives.
(iii) If, in addition, the tuple satisfies the first-order-system identities for every cr ∈K and
every δ ∈[0, δ1], then the endpoint matrices are uniformly invertible, and their inverses
and the maps T = E−(E+)−1 are C1 in cr with uniformly bounded derivatives.
The assumptions hold for the coefficients of §5.2 under Hypotheses (H1) and (H4). Under
these hypotheses, b, ¯ρ0, f, and g2 lie in C3 and g1 lies in C2 as functions of z, with derivatives
that depend continuously on c, because c enters only through V 2 in ¯A. Differentiating the
pullback φ(zj(cr)+x) of such a function φ in cr uses one derivative in z and the C1 dependence
of zj(cr), so the pulled-back tuple is C1 in cr with values in C1([−ς, ς]). The proof treats the
three items in order. For (i), we differentiate the logarithmic term, the remainder integral, and
the regular terms of the pulled-back map separately. For (ii), we differentiate the fixed point of
this affine map, and for (iii), we bound the determinants of the endpoint matrices from below
with the Wronskian.

C
Fr´echet derivative and spectrum of the linearized operator
In this appendix, we prove Theorem 3.5. The theorem settles two points that the derivation in
§3 leaves open.
The first point is the sense in which the system of Proposition 3.1 is the linearization
of the nonlinear two-phase problem. In §3, we obtained this system by differentiating a
one-parameter family of solutions. No well-posedness theory is available for the two-phase
problem (Remark 3.2), so such families are hypothetical. The Fr´echet derivative of the nonlinear
equations at the laminar state does not require solutions, but it requires a map between fixed
Banach spaces, that is, complete normed vector spaces, whereas the air and water domains
move with the interface. In §C.1, we therefore transform the equations to fixed reference
domains and show that the transformed equations are continuously Fr´echet differentiable
(Proposition C.2). In §C.2, we show that a change of variables to a new unknown, the good
unknown, turns this derivative into the linearized system of Proposition 3.1 (Proposition C.3).
Hence that system is the linearization of the nonlinear problem even if the family of solutions
used in §3 does not exist.
The second point is the space on which the linearized operator acts. A differential operator
on L2 is defined only on a subspace of L2, its domain. Its spectrum depends on the choice
of the space and of the domain, and so do the statements that an eigenvalue is isolated and
algebraically simple. The linearized problem also has two features that the domain must respect.
The water pressure has no evolution equation, and the interface and boundary conditions
constrain the state at every time. In §C.3, we define Lk on an L2 space at one wavenumber,
with the constraints in its domain and the water pressure recovered by solving Laplace’s
equation in the water. We then prove that its eigenvalues with Re λ̸ = 0 correspond to the zeros
of the holomorphic dispersion function (Theorem C.6). In §C.4, we show that Lk is the graph
closure of the linearized evolution on the H3 core (Proposition C.7), so that Lk is determined
by the linearized equations and the constraints. In §C.5, we bound the resolvent and show
that a simple zero of E gives an isolated, algebraically simple eigenvalue (Proposition C.9
and Corollary C.10). Corollary 6.6 applies these results to the unstable eigenvalue.
In §C.1, §C.2, and Proposition C.7, we assume, in addition to Hypotheses (H1) and (H2),
the thermodynamic regularity stated in Theorem 3.5. It requires an open set U of states (ρ, S)
63


whose closure U is a compact subset of (0, ∞) × R, with


(188)
The first condition states that U contains the base range, the set of thermodynamic states of
the laminar state. The second condition means that the partial derivatives of P of order at most
five are continuous on U and extend continuously to the closure U . The constant-sound-speed
atmosphere of Example 2.2, whose equation of state is smooth on (0, ∞) × R with Pρ = a2

because a2(0) = γK(S∗)ρ0(0)γ−1 > 0 gives K > 0 near S∗. The C5 regularity of P is used
only in the composition estimates for the Fr´echet derivative (Lemma C.1). The definition of
Lk and its spectral theory for Im c̸ = 0 use only Hypotheses (H1) and (H2). Neutrality gives

C.1
Fixed-domain formulation
Let E1 and E2 be Banach spaces with the norms ∥· ∥E1 and ∥· ∥E2. A linear map G : E1 →E2
is bounded if its operator norm ∥G∥E1→E2 := sup{∥GV∥E2 : V ∈E1, ∥V∥E1 ≤1} is finite, and
then ∥GV∥E2 ≤∥G∥E1→E2∥V∥E1 for all V ∈E1. We write L(E1, E2) for the space of bounded
linear maps from E1 to E2 with the operator norm, L(E1) := L(E1, E1), and ∥G∥for ∥G∥E1→E2
when the spaces are clear from the context. A map F from an open subset of E1 into E2 is
Fr´echet differentiable at a point U if there is dF(U) ∈L(E1, E2), whose value at V ∈E1 we
write dF(U)[V], with ∥F(U + V) −F(U) −dF(U)[V]∥E2 = o(∥V∥E1) as V →0, that is, the
ratio of the left side to ∥V∥E1 tends to zero. We call dF(U) the Fr´echet derivative of F at U
and dF(U)[V] the derivative of F at U in the direction V. The map F is continuously Fr´echet
differentiable, or C1, if it is Fr´echet differentiable at every point and dF depends continuously
on the point in the operator norm. A Fr´echet derivative thus requires a map between fixed
Banach spaces, but the fluid domains move with η. We therefore pull every field back to fixed
reference domains, the horizontal strips Sa and Sw defined below, by a change of variables that
depends on η, a Hanzawa-type transformation [23]. The transformed equations then contain η
and its derivatives in their coefficients, and the interface conditions become conditions on a line
that does not move. However, a perturbation of η alone then changes every pulled-back field,
even when the physical fields do not change. The good unknown of Proposition C.3 removes
this apparent perturbation.
The sets Sa := Tk0 × (0, ha) and Sw := Tk0 × (−hw, 0) are the horizontal strips that the
air and the water occupy when the interface is flat, and we call them the reference strips. We
write (x, ˜z) for the coordinates on them. For a strip S, the space L2(S) consists of the square-
integrable functions on S, with the norm ∥f∥L2(S) :=
 R

The set Ud0 is open, and its closure is contained in the set {y ∈U : dist(y, ∂U ) ≥d0}, which
is a compact subset of U . The set O is open in X, for the following reason. By the Sobolev
embedding theorem in two dimensions [1, Ch. 4], every function f ∈H3 of a strip is continuous
on the closed strip, with ∥f∥L∞≤C∥f∥H3, and the Sobolev embedding theorem on Tk0 gives
∥η∥L∞≤C∥η∥H4(Tk0). The range of a continuous field on the closed strip is compact, and
both defining conditions of O, the range in the open set Ud0 and the strict bound on ∥η∥L∞,
are preserved under perturbations with small L∞norms, hence under perturbations with small
65


norms in X. On O, the density satisfies ˜ρ ≥ρ−> 0, where ρ−is the minimum of ρ over
the closure of Ud0. On this compact set, P, Pρ, and PS are C4 with bounded derivatives,
and Pρ is bounded below by a positive constant. The laminar state in Equation (13) is
U0 := (ρ0, U, 0, S∗; 0, 0, pw,0; 0) in these variables. It lies in X by Hypothesis (H1), and in O
by the choice of d0.

The residual is built from products, quotients, and nonlinear functions of H3 fields, namely
P(˜ρ, ˜S), 1/˜ρ, 1/J, and the curvature of η. Its differentiability in Sobolev spaces rests on
the following standard composition estimates. In the lemma, Cemb denotes the constant of
the Sobolev embedding inequality ∥u∥L∞≤Cemb∥u∥Hm, which holds on a strip for m ≥2
and on Tk0 for m ≥1.
For a compact set K1 in an open set W of R or of R2, we set
r(K1) := min{1, dist(K1, ∂W)}/(2Cemb), where dist(K1, ∂W) is the smallest distance between
a point of K1 and a point of the boundary ∂W of W, and +∞when ∂W is empty.
Lemma C.1. Let S be one of the strips above or the circle Tk0, and let m = 2 or m = 3.
(i) Hm(S) is a Banach algebra, that is, ∥uv∥Hm ≤C∥u∥Hm∥v∥Hm, and the multiplier
inequality ∥uv∥Hm−1 ≤C∥u∥Hm∥v∥Hm−1 holds. On Tk0, the space Hs(Tk0) is a Banach
algebra for every real s > 1
2.

values of u on S. This set of fields u is open, because ran(u), the range of a continuous
field on the compact set S, is a compact subset of W and so has a positive distance to
∂W, and because the embedding inequality ∥h∥L∞≤Cemb∥h∥Hm gives ran(u + h) ⊂W
when Cemb∥h∥Hm is smaller than this distance. For y ∈W and a vector h with as many
components as y, we write dF(y)[h] := P

the functions dF(u)[h] and d2F(u)[h, h] are given by these formulas at each point. The

Proof. For (i), we use the Sobolev embedding inequalities ∥u∥L∞≤C∥u∥H2 and ∥u∥L4 ≤

For m = 2, expand ∂2(uv) by the Leibniz rule and estimate the three types of terms by
∥u∥L∞∥∂2v∥L2, ∥∂u∥L4∥∂v∥L4, and ∥∂2u∥L2∥v∥L∞. For m = 3, the additional terms ∂3u v and

and ∥∂2u∥L4∥∂v∥L4. The multiplier inequality follows the same way, placing the factor from

derivative of the factor in H3 lies in H2 and is therefore bounded. On Tk0, if u and v have the

For (ii), the function F(u) is bounded, and bounded functions lie in L2 because S has finite
area or length. By the chain rule, every term of ∂j[F(u)], 1 ≤j ≤m, is a partial derivative of
F of some order i with 1 ≤i ≤j, evaluated at u and bounded since F ∈Cm on a compact
set, times a product of i derivatives of components of u whose orders add up to j. For m = 2,
this product is a single derivative of order at most two, which lies in L2, or the product of
two first derivatives, which lies in L2 because first derivatives lie in L4 by the embedding
inequality ∥u∥L4 ≤C∥u∥H1. For m = 3, the first derivatives lie in H2 and are therefore
bounded. Since the orders add up to at most three, at most one factor has order two or three,
and this factor lies in L2, so the product is bounded or lies in L2. This uses only F ∈Cm. Let

Proposition C.2. The residual N = (Ndyn, Ncon) is a well-defined map O →Y, and it is
continuously Fr´echet differentiable.
Proof. By Equations (197) and (198), each component of Ndyn on a strip is a finite sum of
products of factors of the following kinds. The first kind is an unknown or a first derivative of an
unknown, which lies in H2. The second kind is one of the functions ϕηx and ϕ′η, which lie in H3
of the strip, since η ∈H4(Tk0) and ϕ is smooth. The third kind is 1/J with J = 1 + ϕ′η, or the
inverse density 1/˜ρ from the momentum components. Both lie in H2 by Lemma C.1(iii) with
m = 2, since J ≥3
4 and ˜ρ ≥ρ−> 0 on O. The fourth kind is one of the compositions Pρ(˜ρ, ˜S)
and PS(˜ρ, ˜S), which appear when the chain rule is applied to ∂η
x ˜pa and ∂η
z ˜pa. They lie in H2
by Lemma C.1(ii) with m = 2 and W = U , because Pρ, PS ∈C4 by P ∈C5. The fifth kind is
68


ext Kη(U), from the last term of Mη. The function Kη(U) of Equation (195) is built from the
traces of ˜u and ˜w at ˜z = 0, which lie in H5/2(Tk0) because the trace map H3(Sa) →H5/2(Tk0)
is bounded, and from ηx ∈H3(Tk0), multiplied in the algebra H5/2(Tk0). By Lemma C.1(iv),
multiplication by its extension is a bounded map on H2 of the strip. Every product of two H2
factors lies in H2 by Lemma C.1(i), so each component of Ndyn on a strip maps O into H2,
and the component [Ndyn]η maps O into H5/2(Tk0). Each factor is a C1 function of U with
values in H2, either because it is linear in U or by Lemma C.1, and finite sums and products of
C1 maps into a Banach algebra are C1. The components of Ncon are treated in the same way,
since the traces are bounded linear maps H3(S) →H5/2(Tk0) and products on Tk0 use the
algebra H5/2(Tk0). In the dynamic condition, P(˜ρ, ˜S) lies in H3(Sa) by Lemma C.1(ii) with
m = 3, since P ∈C5. The curvature κ(η) = −ηxx(1 + η2
x)−3/2 is the product of ηxx ∈H2(Tk0)
and (1 + η2
x)−3/2. The second factor lies in H3(Tk0) by Lemma C.1(ii) with m = 3 and the
smooth function t 7→(1 + t2)−3/2, since ηx ∈H3(Tk0). Hence κ(η) ∈H2(Tk0) by the multiplier
inequality of Lemma C.1(i), and κ is C1 in η for the same reasons. Therefore N maps O into
the space Y of Equation (200) and is C1.
C.2
The Fr´echet derivative in terms of the good unknown
In this subsection, we compute the derivative dN(U0) of the residual at the laminar state and
compare it with the linearized system of Proposition 3.1. The variation of a pulled-back field
at a fixed point of the reference strip differs from the Eulerian variation at the corresponding
physical point. The reason is that a displacement ζ of the interface moves the physical point
Φη(x, ˜z) vertically by ϕ(˜z)ζ(x), so the pulled-back field changes in addition by ϕζ times the
base-state gradient. The good-unknown map below adds exactly this term, as in Alinhac’s
good unknown for free-boundary problems [2], which is used for compressible free boundaries
in [51]. After this change of variables, the derivative of the flattened equations is the Eulerian
linearized system of §3.
Let V = (r, u, w, s; vx, vz, qw; ζ) ∈X be a variation of the Eulerian fields and of the
interface, where we identify the reference strips with the undisturbed domains {0 < z < ha}
and {−hw < z < 0} of §3. We write AV for the tuple of right-hand sides of Equations (34),
(35), and (36), solved for the time derivatives of (r, u, w, s, vx, vz, ζ), with q = a2r + PSs. We
denote its components by [AV]r, . . . , [AV]ζ, so that, for example, [AV]ζ = w|z=0. For V ∈X,
the tuple AV lies in H2(Sa)4 × H2(Sw)2 × H5/2(Tk0), the part of the space Y in Equation (200)
that belongs to the evolution equations.

combination of Equation (37). Thus, in terms of the good unknown, the variations of the
flattened pressures at ˜z = 0 are the Lagrangian pressure variations. The impermeability
conditions at the top and bottom are unchanged by T because ϕ vanishes there. The final
equivalence statement follows because T is invertible, with a bounded inverse, and does not
depend on time.
C.3
The closed linearized operator and its eigenvalues
In this subsection, we define the operator Lk and show that its eigenvalues with Re λ̸ = 0 are
the values λ = −ikc at the zeros of E(k, ·, ε). The coefficients depend only on z, so the linearized
evolution decomposes into spatial Fourier components, and we consider one wavenumber k > 0.
Negative wavenumbers are recovered by the reflection symmetry of Lemma 4.9(i). We define Lk
on the Eulerian Fourier amplitudes and use pressure rather than density as the thermodynamic
variable, because the vertical momentum equation and the dynamic condition involve the
pressure and its derivative. The water pressure is not a component of the state, because it is
determined by the other components.

Lemma C.5. The operator Lk is densely defined and closed on Hk. That is, D(Lk) is dense
in Hk, and the graph {(ˆU, Lk ˆU) : ˆU ∈D(Lk)} is closed in Hk × Hk.
Proof. To prove density, let ˆU ∈Hk and approximate ˆu, ˆq, ˆs in L2 by smooth functions. We keep

and converge to ˆU in Hk.

(ˆu, ˆw, ˆq, ˆs, ˆv, ˆζ) of the normal mode of Proposition 3.3.
In particular every root c of the
dispersion relation in Equation (54) with Im c > 0 gives the eigenvalue λ = −ikc of Lk with
Re λ = k Im c > 0.

For the dimension, the argument above shows that every eigenfunction has the air component
µ (w, ¯χ)(·; k, c) for some µ ∈C, and that its other components are then determined by the
formulas above. Hence the eigenspace is spanned by the eigenfunction with µ = 1.
C.4
The linearized evolution on the constraint space and its closure
In this subsection, we show that Lk is the operator on L2 that the linearized equations of
§C.2 define at wavenumber k, and not only an operator with the same eigenvalues. The
two descriptions differ in three ways. The linearized equations act on the good unknown,
which contains the density and the water pressure, whereas Lk acts on the pressure and does
not contain the water pressure. The linearized equations include the constraint equations of
73


Equation (199), whereas Lk builds them into its space, its domain, and the formula for the water
pressure in Equation (211). In addition, the linearized equations act on H3 states, whereas
Lk acts on L2 states. Proposition C.7 connects the two descriptions. A curve of H3 states
solves the linearized equations and satisfies the constraints exactly when the corresponding
curve of pressure-based states is a classical trajectory of Lk and the water pressure is given
by Equation (211). A classical trajectory of Lk is a curve ˆU(t) in the domain of Lk that is
continuous in the graph norm ∥ˆU∥Hk + ∥Lk ˆU∥Hk of §3.3, continuously differentiable in Hk, and
satisfies
d
dt ˆU = Lk ˆU, as in Proposition C.7(ii). The operator Lk is also the graph closure of the
linearized evolution restricted to the H3 states that satisfy the constraints. We first define the
spaces and maps that connect the two descriptions.
Let Πev be the map that removes the water-pressure component of a tuple, so that ΠevV =
(r, u, w, s, vx, vz, ζ) consists of the components with time derivatives. A C1 curve t 7→T V(t) of
flattened variations satisfies the evolution equations of the linearized flattened system when

if the good unknown solves the Eulerian evolution

therefore compare Lk with the Eulerian evolution in Equation (215).
At wavenumber k, the space Xk consists of the tuples ˆV = (ˆr, ˆu, ˆw, ˆs, ˆvx, ˆvz, ˆqw, ˆζ) with
amplitudes in H3 of their intervals, [0, ha] in the air and [−hw, 0] in the water, and with ˆζ ∈C.
Such a tuple corresponds to the tuple of its components multiplied by eikx. These functions
have complex values and have the period 2π/k0 in x because k = nk0 with a positive integer
n, so their real and imaginary parts are elements of X. The map A has real coefficients and
acts on such a tuple through its real and imaginary parts, and Ak denotes the corresponding
restriction of A, in which ∂x becomes multiplication by ik. The maps Πev and T act on Xk by
the same formulas. The operator Lk uses the pressure ˆq = a2ˆr + PSˆs instead of the density,
and we define the corresponding change of variables

Hk, onto itself with a bounded inverse. Both statements hold because multiplication by a2, by
PS, or by 1/a2 is bounded on H3 and on L2, and the inverse is given by ˆr = (ˆq −PSˆs)/a2.

Remark C.8. Proposition C.7 identifies Lk as the graph closure of Ared
k
on the H3 core, not
of the full evolution Ak. On a state that satisfies the constraints, the constraint equations
determine only the interface value of the water pressure, and the harmonic profile is selected by
the requirement that the evolution preserve the divergence and bottom constraints, as in the
differential-algebraic structure of Equation (196). The image Ared
k
ˆV of a state ˆV ∈Dk need not
itself satisfy the constraints. The conditions that the image also satisfies the constraints are
called higher compatibility conditions, and they concern the time derivative along a classical
trajectory. They are part of the definition of the domains of the powers of Lk, since a state lies
in the domain of L2
k exactly when it lies in D(Lk) and its image under Lk lies in D(Lk) again.
Together with Proposition C.3, Proposition C.7 proves Theorem 3.5(ii).
C.5
The resolvent and the algebraic simplicity of the unstable eigenvalue
In this subsection, we solve the equation (λ −Lk)ˆU = F for λ = −ikc with Im c̸ = 0 and
E(k, c, ε)̸ = 0, and we bound the solution by |E(k, c, ε)|−1 (Proposition C.9). Near a simple
zero of E(k, ·, ε), this bound shows that the eigenvalue is isolated and algebraically simple
(Corollary C.10). We use λ = −ikc, so Re λ = k Im c. The resolvent set of Lk is the set of λ ∈C
for which λ −Lk is a bijection of D(Lk) onto Hk, that is, for which the equation (λ −Lk)ˆU = F
has exactly one solution ˆU ∈D(Lk) for every F ∈Hk. The spectrum of Lk is the complement
of the resolvent set. For λ in the resolvent set, the inverse (λ −Lk)−1 is the resolvent. It is
bounded on Hk by the closed graph theorem, which states that a linear map from a Banach
space into a Banach space that is defined on the whole first space is bounded when its graph
is closed. The graph of (λ −Lk)−1 is the graph of λ −Lk with the two entries of each pair
exchanged, and this graph is closed because Lk is closed.
Proposition C.9. Let k > 0, ε > 0, and a compact set Σ ⊂{c ∈C : Im c̸ = 0} be given.
There is a constant C, which depends on Σ, such that for every c ∈Σ with E(k, c, ε)̸ = 0, the
operator λ −Lk with λ = −ikc is a bijection of D(Lk) onto Hk, so λ lies in the resolvent set
of Lk, and

By Lemma 4.9(ii), a zero c of E(k, ·, ε) with Im c > 0 is the phase speed of a growing normal
mode, whose amplitude grows like ek Im c t. In the next corollary, we mark such a zero and its
eigenvalue with the subscript u, for unstable.
Corollary C.10. Let cu with Im cu > 0 be a zero of E(k, ·, ε) with ∂cE(k, cu, ε)̸ = 0, equivalently,
by Equation (75), a simple root of D(k, ·, ε) at which w(0; k, cu)̸ = 0. Then λu = −ikcu is an
isolated point of the spectrum of Lk, and the resolvent has a pole of first order there, that is,
∥(λ −Lk)−1∥Hk→Hk ≤C/|λ −λu| for λ̸ = λu near λu. For every sufficiently small r > 0, the
Riesz projection
P :=
1
2πi
I
|λ−λu|=r
(λ −Lk)−1 dλ,
(236)
which is the spectral projection onto the eigenvalue λu, has rank one. Hence the eigenvalue is
algebraically simple, and ker
 (Lk −λu)m
= ker(Lk −λu) is one-dimensional for every integer
m ≥1. In particular, under the assumptions of Theorem 6.4, the unstable root cε of Theorem 6.4
is a simple zero of E(k, ·, ε) for every ε ∈(0, ε0) by Theorem 6.4(i) and Equation (75), so the
unstable eigenvalue λε = −ikcε is algebraically simple for every ε ∈(0, ε0).
Proof. The function E(k, ·, ε) is holomorphic near cu (Lemma 4.9) and has a simple zero
there. By Taylor’s theorem, there is r1 ∈(0, Im cu) with |E(k, c, ε)| ≥1
2|∂cE(k, cu, ε)| |c −cu|
for |c −cu| ≤r1, and these c lie in the upper half-plane. We apply Proposition C.9 with
Σ = {c ∈C : |c −cu| ≤r1} and use |λ −λu| = k|c −cu|. Then every λ̸ = λu with |λ −λu| ≤kr1
lies in the resolvent set of Lk, and ∥(λ −Lk)−1∥≤C/|λ −λu|. Since λu is an eigenvalue
by Theorem C.6, it is an isolated point of the spectrum. The resolvent is holomorphic for
0 < |λ −λu| ≤kr1, and the coefficients of the negative powers (λ −λu)−n, n ≥1, in its Laurent
expansion about λu, the expansion in positive and negative powers of λ −λu, are
Pn :=
1
2πi
I
|λ−λu|=r
(λ −λu)n−1(λ −Lk)−1 dλ,
0 < r ≤kr1,
(237)
so that P = P1. The circle has length 2πr, the factor |λ −λu|n−1 equals rn−1 on it, and
the operator norm of the resolvent is at most C/r on it, so ∥Pn∥≤Cr n−1 for every such r.
Letting r →0 gives Pn = 0 for n ≥2, so the pole has order one. For a closed operator with
an isolated spectral point, P is a projection that commutes with Lk on D(Lk) [26, Ch. III,
§6.5]. A first-order pole makes λu semisimple, that is, every generalized eigenvector, a vector
ˆU with (Lk −λu)m ˆU = 0 for some m ≥1, is an eigenvector. We verify this and the rank
directly. For the resolvent (λ −Lk)−1, the Laurent coefficients satisfy (Lk −λu)Pn = Pn+1, so
(Lk −λu)P = P2 = 0 [26, Ch. III, §6.5]. Hence the range ran P of P is contained in ker(Lk −λu),
which is one-dimensional by Theorem C.6. The projection P acts as the identity on this null
space, because every ˆU ∈ker(Lk −λu) satisfies (λ −Lk)−1 ˆU = (λ −λu)−1 ˆU, so PˆU = ˆU. Hence
ran P = ker(Lk −λu), and P has rank one. It remains to exclude generalized eigenvectors
79


that are not eigenvectors. In particular, the domain D
 (Lk −λu)2
of the square is the set
of ˆU ∈D(Lk) with (Lk −λu)ˆU ∈D(Lk). If ˆU lies in this set and (Lk −λu)2 ˆU = 0, put
ˆW := (Lk −λu)ˆU ∈ker(Lk −λu). Then ˆW = P ˆW = P(Lk −λu)ˆU = (Lk −λu)PˆU = 0, because
P commutes with Lk on its domain and (Lk −λu)P = 0. So ker
 (Lk −λu)2
= ker(Lk −λu),
and by induction the same holds for every power.
The same argument applies at a simple zero c of E(k, ·, ε) with Im c < 0. Alternatively, such
zeros are the complex conjugates of zeros in the upper half-plane, by the reflection symmetry
of Lemma 4.9(i).
This completes the proof of Theorem 3.5. Part (i) is Propositions C.2 and C.3, part
(ii) is Lemma C.5 and Proposition C.7 with Proposition C.3, and part (iii) is Theorem C.6,
Proposition C.9, and Corollary C.10.
Remark C.11. The linearized system on Tk0 decomposes over the Fourier modes k = nk0,
n ∈Z, and the components with n < 0 are given by the same formulas. The mean-flow
component n = 0 is not constructed in this paper. An operator for the full linearized system,
which acts on each Fourier mode separately, can be assembled from these components once the
component n = 0 is defined on its space of states that satisfy the constraints. For every such
operator, the eigenfunction of Lk constructed above, multiplied by eikx, is an L2 eigenfunction,
and under the assumptions of Theorem 6.4 it has the eigenvalue λε = −ikcε with Re λε > 0
(Corollary 6.6).
Remark C.12. The resolvent is constructed only for Im c̸ = 0, that is, for Re λ̸ = 0. The set
{−ikU(z) : 0 ≤z ≤ha} of values of the coefficient −ikU in Lk, which corresponds to the real
phase speeds in the range of U, is the candidate for the continuous spectrum, the set of λ
that are not eigenvalues and for which the equation (λ −Lk)ˆU = F can be solved for a dense
set of F but not for every F. A limiting-absorption estimate for (λ −Lk)−1 as λ approaches
this set would require an inhomogeneous version of Theorem 5.8 and is not established here.
Semigroup generation is not addressed (Remark 3.2).
